Combinatorics of cyclic-conditional freeness Thanks: O.A. gratefully acknowledges financial support by the grants Conacyt A1-S-9764 and SFB TRR 195. Thanks: G.C. is supported by the Project MESA (ANR-18-CE40-006) and by the Project STARS (ANR-20-CE40-0008) of the French National Research Agency (ANR) Thanks: N.G. is supported by the Project STARS (ANR-20-CE40-0008) of the French National Research Agency (ANR)
Octavio Arizmendi †, Guillaume Cébron ⋆, Nicolas Gilliers ⋆,⋄

Abstract
. We develop the relevant combinatorics pertaining to cyclic-conditional freeness in order to introduce the adequate sets of cumulants linearizing the cyclic-conditional additive convolution. On our way, we introduce a new non-commutative independence, cyclic freeness . We explain how cyclic-conditional freeness is “reduced” to cyclic freeness by utilizing a multivariate extension of the inverse Markov-Krein transform . We also consider cyclic-conditional multiplicative convolution and prove limit theorems.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
46L53, 46L54

1.
Introduction

Non-commutative probability is an area of Operators Algebra Theory that tries the subject from the point of view of probability, see
[
33
]
. In this sense, one considers bounded operators in a von Neumann algebra or unbounded operators affiliated to a von Neuman algebra acting on a pointed Hilbert space as random variables. One associates a distribution to these random variables depending on a linear functional (the state) on the algebra these operators generate.

One of the essential concepts of probability is independence since it allows the building of families of random variables with a natural and manageable joint distribution. Many theories, such as stochastic processes, particularly Markov processes, are based on independence.
A surprising feature of non-commutative probability is the possibility of
several
(abstract) independences
1
1

1

see
https://nci-wiki.apps.math.cnrs.fr/start
for a growing list
in opposition to classical probability where independence between classical random variables is somewhat unambiguous. Even more interesting, the same collection of variables can have
multiple
joint distributions, depending on the functionals being considered for computing expectations. These various non-commutative distributions can interact coherently; one speaks of independence between
tuples of non-commutative distributions
: two-states independences, three-states independences and so-on. Two epitomic examples of two-states independences for the present work are, firstly, the conditional freeness
[
7
,
8
]
of Bożejko, Leinert and Speicher and secondly the infinitesimal freeness
[
14
]
of Février and Nica.

Introducing a non-commutative independence between operators can be done in several ways, following for various reasons, tracking more or less convincing purposes. One can legitimately wonder which of them are fundamental. Two approaches, which do not exclude the other, may help to decide this matter. The first comes from imposing axioms to define natural or universal independence, and the second comes from examples. Apart from the abstract examples from Operator Theory, two other more concrete areas of mathematics are sources of examples worthy of study with applications in mind.
On the one hand, Random Matrix Theory has broadly benefited from Voiculescu’s free probability
[
37
]
since many matrix ensembles in the high-dimensional limit display asymptotic freeness
[
32
,
39
]
.
More recently, other independences have appeared in connections with fine-grain limiting behaviour of spectra of random matrices, such as the already mentioned infinitesimal freeness
[
9
,
12
,
31
]
.

Cyclic-monotone independence
[
11
]
was specifically designed to study random matrix models with purely discrete eigenvalues in the high-dimensional limit. In
[
2
]
, the first author, Hasebe and Lehner introduced an operatorial model to cyclic-monotone independence, which they leverage to introduce the new concept of cyclic-boolean independence.
In
[
10
]
, the two last authors proposed a random matrix model, the
Vortex Model
displaying conditional freeness in the high dimensional limit. They computed the fluctuations of the empirical spectral values distribution in this convergence with the help of the appropriate independence original to their work, which they named
cyclic-conditional freeness
.

In non-commutative probability, efficient computational tools are essential to avoid often intractable computations. Cumulants are at the basis of a neat algorithmic answer to the problem of computing the distribution of the sum of independent random variables.
To provide the corresponding set of cumulants is fundamental when a new independence crops up. We tackle this in the present work, for the cyclic-conditional freeness.
In defining these cumulants, we take as the first step the reduction of cyclic-conditional freeness, which is a three-state independence, to a two-state independence that we call cyclic freeness.
Surprisingly, this reduction utilizes a multivariate extension of the celebrated (inverse) Markov-Krein transform. Cyclic freeness coincides with infinitesimal freeness when the two states are tracial linear functionals. We leverage this observation to build upon the intuition gained in the work
[
14
]
of Février and Nica about infinitesimally free cumulants, the cyclically-free cumulants.
Finally, we establish the basic properties of these cumulants, and we provide subordination relations between the appropriate transforms, thus providing an effective way of computing cyclic-conditional additive and multiplicative convolutions.

Let us mention two additional motivations for the introduction of the cyclic freeness. Firstly, this new independence allows to extend the connection between conditional and infinitesimal freeness found by Février, Mastnak, Nica and Szpojankowski
[
13
]
to non-tracial states. Secondly, cyclic-conditional freeness on one hand and cyclic freeness, cylic-monotone and cylic-Boolean independences on the other hand have relation reminiscent to the ones holding between conditional freeness and the triptych of the monotone, Boolean and free independences
[
15
,
7
]
(see Fig.
1
).

cyclic-conditional freeness
cyclic Boolean
cyclic freeness
cyclic monotone
Boolean independence
freeness
monotone independence
conditional freeness

Figure 1.
Each arrow means that the initial independence generalizes the terminal one.

1.1.
Main Contributions

(1)

We introduce a new independence we call
cyclic freeness
for random variables in a non-commutative probability space endowed with two, possibly non-unital, linear functional of which one is tracial. Under an additional hypothesis, we prove that cyclic freeness coincides with the
infinitesimal freeness
of Février and Nica
[
14
]
. By using exchangeability systems
[
21
]
, we provide cumulants linearizing the corresponding additive convolution.

(2)

We give to a non-commutative probability space and an independence (among free, monotone, Boolean and conditional freeness)
a cyclic companion
: a (non-commutative) cyclic probability space such that independence in the former implies the corresponding cyclic independence in the latter. Besides, we recognize in this construction the role of an extension of the inverse Markov-Krein transform to the joint distribution of a family of random variables.

(3)

We develop the combinatorial side of
cyclic-conditional freeness
proposed by the last two authors in
[
10
]
with the definition of corresponding sequences of cumulants. We prove moments-cumulants relations and the characterization of cyclic-conditional freeness between random variables via the vanishing of their mixed cumulants. Our strategy to introducing these cumulants implies reduction of cyclic-conditional freeness to cyclic freeness. We first consider the companion cyclic-conditional probability space which provides an additional triple with respect to which cyclic-conditional freeness with respect to the initial triple implies cyclic-conditional freeness with respect to this companion triple of states. For these triples, the two first state coincide, and we use, in a second time, the important observation that by taking the difference between the third functionals in these triples yields, with the common first states of these triples, a pair of functionals with respect to which cyclic freeness is implied by cyclic-conditional freeness (with respect to the initial state). In a third final stage we introduce cyclic-conditional cumulants as the cyclic free cumulants with respect to this pair.

1.2.
Outline

(1)

In Section
1.3
, we collect the basic definitions pertaining to various non-commutative independences including the conditional freeness of Bożejko, Leinert and Speicher. The knowledgeable reader can skip this part.

(2)

In Section
2.1
, we give the definition of cyclic freeness (see Definition
3.1.2
). In Section
2.2
, we exhibit a connection between conditional and cyclic freeness, see Theorem
2.2.2
which extends a result of Février, Mastnak, Nica and Szpojankowski
[
13
]
.
The cyclic free cumulants can be found in Section
2.4
. The combinatorial characterization of cyclic freeness is proved in Theorem
2.4.10
.

(3)

In Section
3
, we define cyclic-conditional freeness, see Definition
3.1.2
. We introduce the adapted sets of cumulants in Section
3.3
, Definition
3.3.1
. In Proposition
3.2.2
of Section
3.2
, we show that a generalization of the inverse Markov-Krein transform yields a link between conditional freeness and cyclic-conditional freeness.

We adopt the following notations:

(1)

If
S
is a set, we denote by
\mathbb{C}\langle S\rangle
the free unital algebra on
S
.

(2)

Let
n\geq 1
, we notate
[n]
the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

(3)

If
\mathcal{A}
is an unital complex algebra we notate
T(\mathcal{A})
the tensor algebra
T(\mathcal{A})=\mathbb{C}1\oplus\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}
over
\mathcal{A}
, with multiplication given by
\otimes
.

1.3.
Background: non-commutative independences

We recall elementary definitions of the three one-state non-commutative “natural independences” in the setting of a non-commutative probability space (we discard classical independence) and the related sets of cumulants and posets guiding the underlying combinatorics of the non-commutative moments-cumulant relations.
A
non-commutative probability space
is a pair of unital algebra
\mathcal{A}
and a unital linear functional
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
. Since the goal of this Section is to expose the combinatorics hinging at the definition of cumulants, we will not make any further assumptions about the topology of
\mathcal{A}
(and largely ignore assumptions about positivity and hermitianity).
In this short exposition, we give an account of the extension of Rota’s perspective on classical cumulants to build cumulants for the natural non-commutative independences.
In particular, we exhibit in each case a poset, a sub-algebra of functions for the incidence algebra of the poset (called
multiplicative functions
) on this poset and perform Möbius inversion. The situation is a bit different for the conditionally free cumulants.

We refer the reader to
[
26
]
for a detailed account of
freeness
and on
free cumulants
. Let
n\geq 1
be an integer. Recall that a partition of
[n]
is a collection of non-empty disjoint sets covering
[n]
. The set of all partitions of
[n]
is denoted
\mathcal{P}(n)
. We notate
{\rm{\rm NC}}(n)
the set of all
non-crossing partitions
of the interval
[n]
: a partition of
[n]
is non-crossing if and only

a,b,c,d\in[n],\penalty\ a<b<c<d,\penalty\ a\sim_{\pi}c,\penalty\ b\sim_{\pi}d\implies b\sim_{\pi}c

where, for
a,b\in[n]
,
a\sim_{\pi}b
means that
a
and
b
are elements of the same block of
\pi
.
We use the notation
\textbf{}{1}_{n}
for the one-block partition
\{\{1,\ldots,n\}\}
and
{0}_{n}
for the partition
\{\{1\},\ldots,\{n\}\}
.

The
containment order
on
\mathcal{P}(n)
is denoted with the symbol
\prec
: given two non-crossing partitions
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(n)
,
\pi_{1}\prec\pi_{2}
if and only if every block of
\pi_{1}
is contained in a block of
\pi_{2}
. We will frequently use the following notation, with
V=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{l}\}\subset[n]
and
a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}
,

a_{V}:=a_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i_{l}}.

The subset
V
is implicitly endowed with the poset structure obtained by restricting the natural linear order of
[n]
to
V
. This will not always be the case and we will make as explicit as possible the order we consider on
V
any time confusion may arise.

Given two sub-algebras
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
of a probability space
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
, a sequence of elements
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
in
\mathcal{A}
with
a_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{j}}
,
i_{j}\in\{1,2\}
is
alternated
if for all
1\leq j<n
,
i_{j}\neq i_{j+1}
. The sequence is
centered
with respect to a linear functional if each
a_{i}
belongs to the kernel of this linear functional.

Finally, we recall the definition of the Kreweras complement
[
20
]
of a non-crossing partition.
Let
\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)
be a non-crossing partition. The Kreweras complement of
\pi
, notated
{\rm Kr}(\pi)
, is the largest partition of the set
\{1^{\prime},2^{\prime},\ldots,n^{\prime}\}
among the partitions
\beta\in{\rm NC}(\{1^{\prime},\ldots,n^{\prime}\})
such that, with natural identifications,

\pi\cup\beta
is a non-crossing partition of
\{1,1^{\prime},\ldots,n,n^{\prime}\}
.

There are two very common ways of representing a non-crossing partitions, emphasizing either the linear order of
[n]
or the canonical cyclic order of
[n]
. In this latter view, a non-crossing partitions corresponds to a system of non-intersecting arcs connecting labeled dots disposed on a circumference, see Figure
2
. In this picture, the blocks of the Kreweras complement are the connected areas bordered by arcs of the blocks of the partitions and bits of the circumference between two consecutive dots.

Figure 2.
The non-crossing partition
\{\{1,4,8\},\{2\},\{3\},\{5,7\},\{6\},\{9,13,14\},\{10,12\},\{11\},\{15,16\}\}
and a block of its Kreweras complement coloured in red. In blue, the outer blocks of the partitions

1.3.1 Definition

(Freeness, Voiculescu
[
37
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
be a non-commutative probability space.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\subset\mathcal{A}
be two
unital
sub-algebras. The two sub-algebras
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are
free
with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
if, for any sequence
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
of
\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
which is
alternating
and
centered
with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
, we have

(1)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=0.

1.3.2 Remark
.

Thanks to Lemma 5.18 in
[
26
]
, the condition (
1
) can be replaced by the following one: for any centered alternating words
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
and
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})
, we have

(2)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n}a_{1}\cdots a_{m})={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}a_{m}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{2}a_{m-1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{n}a_{1})

if
n=m
is even and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n}a_{1}\cdots a_{m})
otherwise.

Free cumulants give a computationally efficient way of checking the freeness of two algebras than the conditions (
1
) on moments and we recall their definition now
[
26
]
.
A sequence of linear maps
f_{n}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C},\penalty\ n\geq\penalty\ 1
, yields
multiplicative functions

f_{\pi}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C}
on the posets
{\rm NC}(n)
of non-crossing partitions,
\pi\in{\rm NC}(n),n\geq 1
:

(3)

f_{\pi}(a_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{n})=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\pi\\
V=(i_{1}<\ldots<i_{\ell})\end{subarray}}f_{\ell}(a_{i_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{i_{\ell}}).

We use the terminology multiplicative to indicate that
f_{\pi}
factorizes over the blocks of the partition
\pi
. We shall discuss a variation of this property in the context of cyclic freeness.

1.3.3 Definition

(
[
26
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
be a non-commutative probability space. The free cumulants
\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C},\penalty\ {n\geq 1}
are defined by the following inductive formula, for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
,

(4)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{n}(a_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{\pi\neq\mathbb{1}_{n}\in{\rm NC}(n)}\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{n}).

1.3.4 Theorem

(
[
26
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
be two unital sub-algebras of
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
. Freeness of
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
is equivalent to the following vanishing of the mixed free cumulants
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 2}
: for any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
with

a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2},\exists 1\leq i\neq j\leq n,\penalty\ a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{1},\penalty\ a_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{2},

one has

\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{n}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=0.

In
[
34
]
, the authors introduce Boolean independence and the related set of cumulants.
Let
n\neq 1
be an integer. In this section, we denote by
\mathcal{I}(n)
the set of all
interval partitions
of
[n]
: a partition
\pi
of
[n]
is in
\mathcal{I}(n)
if and only if the blocks of
\pi
are intervals (for the natural linear order on
[n]=\{1<\ldots<n\})
. A sequence of linear maps
f_{n}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C},\penalty\ n\geq 1
yields a family of linear maps
f_{I}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C},\penalty\ I\in\mathcal{I}(n),\penalty\ n\geq 1
indexed by interval partitions, for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
:

f_{I}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})=\prod_{V=(i_{1}<\ldots<i_{l})\in I}f_{\ell}(a_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i_{l}}).

1.3.5 Definition
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complex algebra equipped with a linear functional
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\subset\mathcal{A}
be two sub-algebras. The two sub-algebras
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are
Boolean independent
with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
if, for any alternating sequence
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
of
\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{n}).

1.3.6 Definition

(Boolean independence, Speicher-Woroudi
[
34
]
and the references therein)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})
be a non-commutative probability space. The Boolean cumulants
(\beta_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C})_{n\geq 1}
are defined by the the following inductive definition

(5)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=\beta_{1_{n}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(a_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{I}(n)\end{subarray}\\
pi\neq 1_{n}}\beta_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(a_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{n}).

1.3.7 Theorem

(
[
34
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
be two sub-algebras of
\mathcal{A}
. Boolean independence of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}
is equivalent to the following vanishing of mixed Boolean cumulants
(\beta_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})_{n\geq 2}
: for any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
with

a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2},\exists 1\leq i\neq j\leq n,\penalty\ a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{1},\penalty\ a_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{2},

one has

\beta_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(a_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{n})=0.

The final one-state non-commutative independence we will consider (and the only one remaining to, see
[
25
]
) is monotone independence, introduced by Muraki in
[
24
]
with the corresponding set of cumulants introduced in
[
17
]
. Recall that contrary to the free and Boolean independences, monotone independence is
not
equivalent to the vanishing of monotone cumulants.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer. We denote by
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{P}(n)
the set of
ordered partitions

[
17
]
on
[n]
; pairs
(\pi,\lessdot)
of a partition
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(n)
and a total order
\lessdot
on
\pi
. Note that a non-crossing partition
\pi
carries a canonical
partial order
provided by the
nesting
between blocks: a block
V
of
\pi
is less than a second block
W
of
\pi
for that order if
W
is contained in the convex hull (for the natural order on
[n]
) of the block
V
.
A
monotone partition
is an ordered partition
(\pi,\lessdot)
equipped with a total order
\lessdot
compatible with the nesting order: if
W
is less than
V
in the nesting order then
W\lessdot V
.
The set of all monotone partitions is denoted
\mathcal{M}(n)
.
As is usual now, a sequence of linear maps
f_{n}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C}
yields a family of linear maps
f_{(\pi,\lessdot)}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C}
,
(\pi,\lessdot)\in\mathcal{M}(n)
indexed by monotone partitions:

f_{\pi}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\prod_{V\in\pi}f_{|V|}(a_{V}).

1.3.8 Definition

(Monotone independence, Muraki
[
24
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
be a non-commutative probability space. Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
be two sub-algebras of
\mathcal{A}
. We say that
\mathcal{A}
is monotonic independent from
\mathcal{A}_{2}
if for any alternating sequence
(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})
with
a_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{k}}
,
i_{k}\in\{1,2\}

(i_{k}\neq i_{k+1})
, and
p\in[n]

with
i_{p-1}<i_{p}>i_{p+1}
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{p-1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{p})a_{p+1}\cdots a_{n}).

1.3.9 Definition

(Hasebe
[
17
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
be a non-commutative probability space. The
monotone cumulants

K^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{n}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C}
of
\mathcal{A}
are recursively defined by the following formula

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=K^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{n}(a_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{(\pi,\lessdot),\pi\neq\mathbb{1}_{n}\in\mathcal{M}(n)}\frac{1}{|\pi|!}\prod_{V\in\pi}K_{\pi}(a_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{n})

We now come to the first multi-states independence we will consider; a kind of freeness but between sub-algebras of an unital algebra equipped with a
pair of states
; a conditional probability space.

1.3.10 Definition

(Conditional probability space)
.

A conditional probability space is a triple
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
where

(1)

\mathcal{A}
an unital algebra,

(2)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
are two unital linear functionals.

1.3.11 Definition

(Conditional freeness
[
7
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
be a conditional probability space. Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
be two unital sub-algebras of
\mathcal{A}
. We say that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are
conditionally free
if for any centered with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
and alternating sequence
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
of elements in
\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=0\textrm{ and }{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(a_{1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(a_{n}).

For the three non-commutative natural independences, the sets of cumulants we defined are the unique multi-linear functionals satisfying some very natural assumptions, see
[
21
]
. For multi-state independences, we need to consider multiple sequences of cumulants to characterize independence through their vanishing, and any not pathological linear combinations of them will fit this requirement. We will present the sequences of cumulants introduced by Bożejko, Leinert and Speicher, which can be also produced by the machinery of Lehner based on Good’s formula and exchangeability systems. These sequences of cumulants have nice properties, however, they fail to satisfy the Krawczyk and Speicher identity
[
19
]
. This identity can be enforced by taking a simple linear combination of the sequences introduced by Bożejko, Leinert and Speicher, as demonstrated by Cabanal-Duvillard
2
2

2

In an unpublished work.
https://helios2.mi.parisdescartes.fr/~cabanal/Articles/NCcondition.html
. We will observe a similar phenomenon in the definition of cumulants for the cyclic-conditional freeness; a linear combination of the cumulants produced by Lehner’s machinery is better behaved.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer. Given a non-crossing partition
\pi\in{\rm{\rm NC}}(n)
, a maximum element for the nesting order on
\pi
(it is not contained in the convex hull of any other block) will be called an
outer block
. A block of
\pi
that is not an outer block is called
inner block
. We use the notations
{\rm Out}(\pi)
for the subset of outer blocks and set
{\rm Inner}(\pi)=\pi\backslash{\rm Outer}(\pi)
. In Fig
2
, we painted in blue the outer blocks of the partition.
Two sequences of linear functions
f_{n},g_{n}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C},\penalty\ n\geq 1
yield a family of maps
[f,g]_{\pi},\pi\in{\rm NC}(n),\penalty\ n\geq 1
indexed by non-crossing partitions, for
\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)
:

[f,g]_{\pi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=\prod_{O\in{\rm Out}(\pi)}f_{|O|}(a_{O})\prod_{V\in{\rm Inner}(\pi)}g_{|V|}(a_{V}).

1.3.12 Definition

(
[
7
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
be an conditional non-commutative probability space. The conditional cumulants of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}
respectively to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is the sequence of linear maps
\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}
that is recursively defined by the following formula:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})

\displaystyle=\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{\pi\in{\rm{\rm NC}}(n)}\prod_{O\in{\rm Outer}(\pi)}\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{O})\prod_{V\in{\rm Inner}(\pi)}\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(a_{V})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in{\rm{\rm NC}}(n)}[\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}]_{\pi}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}).

1.3.13 Theorem

(
[
7
]
)
.

Conditional freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
is equivalent to the vanishing of mixed free cumulants
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
and of the mixed conditionally free cumulants
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
.

We note that the definitions of inner and outer blocks of a partition
\pi
depends on the canonical linear order of
[n]=\{1<2<\cdots<n\}
.

2.
Infinitesimal and cyclic freeness

We propose
cyclic freeness
, a non-commutative independence prescribing joint moments computed with respect to a pair of an unital linear functional (notated
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
in the following) and a tracial, possibly non-unital, linear functional (notated
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
in the following). If the first functional is
tracial
and the second one is equal to
0
on the unit of the algebra, cyclic freeness is the
infinitesimal freeness
defined by Belinschi and Shlyakhtenko
[
4
]
.

2.1.
Infinitesimal and cyclic freeness

2.1.1 Definition

(Infinitesimal freeness, see
[
14
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
be a non-commutative probability space. For any linear functional
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
, two unital sub-algebras
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are called
infinitesimally free
with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
whenever, for any sequence
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
of
\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
which is alternating and centered with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
, we have

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{i-1}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{i})\cdot a_{i+1}\cdots a_{n}).

Our general definition of infinitesimal freeness differs slightly from the original definition in that we do not assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
vanishes on the unit of
\mathcal{A}
. In particular, with
\delta={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})
, and
a,b
in two different infinitesimally free subalgebras with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(ab)=-\delta{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(b)+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(b)+{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(b).

2.1.2 Remark
.

(1)

The second relation on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\ldots a_{n})
is equivalent, under the same conditions on
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
, to

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\ldots a_{n})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}a_{n}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{2}a_{n-1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{\frac{n+1}{2}})&{\rm if}\penalty\ i_{1}=i_{n},i_{2}=i_{n-1},\ldots,i_{\frac{n-1}{2}}=i_{\frac{n+3}{2}}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(2)

Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
. If
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are infinitesimally free with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{1})

and
with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{2})
, then
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are infinitesimally free with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\alpha_{1}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{1}+\alpha_{2}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{2})
.

(3)

When
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=0
, infinitesimal freeness for the couple
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
is equivalent to freeness for a conditional expectation taking values in the Grassman algebra
\mathbb{G}
and the scalars of
\mathcal{A}
augmented to
\mathbb{G}
see
[
14
, Chapter 4]
and
[
35
]
.

We move to the definition of cyclic freeness, which originates from
[
10
, Proposition 3.2.4]
.

2.1.3 Definition

(Cyclic freeness)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
be a non-commutative probability space. For any tracial linear functional
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
, two unital sub-algebras
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are called
infinitesimally free
with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
whenever they are free with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
and, for any sequence
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
in
\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
which is
cyclically alternating
(
n\geq 2
) and centered with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=0.

Equivalently, for any sequence
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
,
n\geq 1
of
\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
which is
alternating
and centered with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
, we have

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\text{and}\ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{i+1}\cdots a_{n}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{i})\cdot a_{1}\cdots a_{i-1}).

By inserting the moments condition characterizing freeness of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
in the second equation above, cyclic freeness is equivalent to

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=0

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{n}a_{1}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{n-1}a_{2})\cdots{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{\frac{n+3}{2}}a_{\frac{n-1}{2}}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{\frac{n+1}{2}})&{\rm if}\penalty\ i_{1}=i_{n},i_{2}=i_{n-1},\ldots,i_{\frac{n-1}{2}}=i_{\frac{n+3}{2}}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Notice the inversions in the products
a_{n}a_{1}
,
a_{n-1}a_{2},\ldots
in comparison to the characterization of infinitesimal freeness given in point (
1
).

2.1.4 Example
.

With
\delta=\partial{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})
, and
a\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
b\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
cyclically free,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(aba)={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(ba^{2})=-\delta{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(b){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a^{2})+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(b){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a^{2})+{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(b){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a^{2}).

2.1.5 Remark
.

When
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is tracial, the above moments conditions for cyclic freeness equal the moment conditions of infinitesimal freeness (
1
). One can even only require that the restrictions of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
to each sub-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are tracial: in that case too, cyclic freeness between
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is equivalent to infinitesimal freeness. This holds in particular when
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are each generated by one variable.

The moment condition for cyclic freeness is linear on the functional
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
. This simple observation is stated more formally in the following proposition and we omit the proof.

2.1.6 Proposition
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
. If
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are cyclically free with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{1})

and
with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{2})
, then
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are cyclically free with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\alpha_{1}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{1}+\alpha_{2}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{2})
.

2.2.
From conditional to infinitesimal and cyclic freeness

We present now a connection between conditional freeness and infinitesimal/cyclic freeness. We let

(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
be a non-commutative probability space and suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}
is tracial.
Following a remark by Belinschi and Shlyakhtenko, the authors Février, Mastnak, Nica and Szpojankowski introduced in
[
13
]
the tracial linear map

W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}):\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}

defined by, for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
:

(6)

\displaystyle W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})(x_{a_{1}}\cdots x_{a_{n}})=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(a_{i},a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i}),

\displaystyle W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})(1)=0.

Defining
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}:\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}
, for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
by

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(x_{a_{1}}\cdots x_{a_{n}})={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n}),\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(1)=1,

we get
an infinitesimal probability space

(\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\rangle,({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})))
. The result of Février, Mastnak, Nica and Szpojankowski can be stated as follows.

2.2.1 Theorem

(Theorem 1.3 and 1.4 in
[
13
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital complex algebra equipped with two unital linear functionals
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
where
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is
tracial
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\subset\mathcal{A}
be two unital
conditionally free
sub-algebras in the conditional probability space
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
. Then, the two algebras
\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\rangle
and
\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}_{2}\rangle
are two infinitesimally free sub-algebras (or equivalently cyclic free) of
(\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\rangle,{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}))
,

We extend the previous theorem when
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is not tracial.

2.2.2 Theorem
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an unital complex algebra equipped with two unital linear functionals
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\subset\mathcal{A}
be two unital sub-algebras which are conditionally free with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
. Then,
\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\rangle
and
\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}_{2}\rangle
are two sub-algebras of
\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\rangle
which are cyclically free with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}))
, where
W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
is given by (
6
).

Proof.

Because
W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
is tracial, it suffices to prove that, for any sequence
(P_{1},\ldots,P_{N})
(
N\geq 2
) with
P_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\rangle
which is cyclically alternating in
\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\rangle
and
\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}_{2}\rangle
, and centered with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
, we have

W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})(P_{1}\cdots P_{N})=0.

For convenience of writing, we will assume that
P_{j}
is a monomial, but everything can be extended to the general case by the multilinearity of all functionals involved. We rewrite the full product as

P_{1}\cdots P_{N}=x_{a_{1}}\cdots x_{a_{n}},

with
P_{k}=\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in I_{k}}x_{a_{i}}
for a certain interval partition
[n]=I_{1}\cup\cdots\cup I_{N}
. We have
\mathcal{A}_{j(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{j(N)}\in\{\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\}
in such a way that
i\in I_{k}\Rightarrow a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{j(k)}
and the cyclic alternating condition means that
j(k)\neq j(k+1)
for
1\leq k\leq N
(with the convention
N+1=1
). Then

W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})(P_{1}\cdots P_{N})=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(a_{i},a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i}),

and it remains to prove now that, for any
i\in[n]
, we have

\beta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(a_{i},a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i})=0,

which a consequence of the following Lemma
2.2.3
.
∎

2.2.3 Lemma
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital complex algebra equipped with two unital linear functionals
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\subset\mathcal{A}
be two unital sub-algebras which are conditionally free with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
.

We consider
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
such that
a_{1}\cdots a_{n}
can be written as some cyclically alternated product of centered elements, i.e. for a certain interval partition
[n]=I_{1}\cup\cdots\cup I_{N}
such that
i\in I_{k}\Rightarrow a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{j(k)}
with
j(1),\ldots,j(N)\in\{1,2\}

\scriptstyle\bullet

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\left(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in I_{k}}a_{i}\right)=0
;

\scriptstyle\bullet

j(k)\neq j(k+1)
for
1\leq k\leq N
(with the convention
N+1=1
).

Then, for any
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
, we have

(7)

\beta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(a_{j},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i})=0.

Proof.

Set
r:=n+1-j+i
.
We have

\displaystyle\beta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(a_{j},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\rm NC}(r)\\
1\sim_{\pi}r\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{j},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\subset[r]\\
\{1,r\}\subset V\end{subarray}}\kappa_{V}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{j},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\rm NC}(r)\\
V\in\pi\end{subarray}}\prod_{W\in\pi,W\neq V}\kappa_{W}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{j},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i}).

Owing to conditional freeness of the algebras
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
,
\kappa_{V}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{j},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i})=0
whenever the variables do not belongs to the same algebra. So we can restrict the sum to the subset
V
which connect variables
a_{k}
’s in the same algebra (let say
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, as the other case is similar).
For a fixed
V\subset[r]
containing
1
and
r
and connecting
a_{k}
’s in the same algebra
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, we decompose
[r]\setminus V
in (maximal) intervals
W_{1},\ldots,W_{\ell}
in such a way that the sum of free cumulants yields
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
on each interval:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\rm NC}(r)\\
V\in\pi\end{subarray}}\prod_{W\in\pi,W\neq V}\kappa_{W}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{j},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i})

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=1}^{\ell}{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}_{W_{m}}(a_{j},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i}).

Now, this product is vanishing
because at least one interval of
a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1}
contains a variable in
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, leading to a term

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}_{W_{m}}(a_{j},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i})={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(\underbrace{\vphantom{\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in I_{t+1}}a_{i}}\cdots}_{\in\mathcal{A}_{1}}\underbrace{\vphantom{\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in I_{t+1}}a_{i}}\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in I_{t}}a_{i}}_{\in\mathcal{A}_{2}}\cdot\underbrace{\vphantom{a_{r+1}}\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in I_{t+1}}a_{i}}_{\in\mathcal{A}_{1}}\cdots\underbrace{\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in I_{t+s}}a_{i}}_{\in\mathcal{A}_{2}}\underbrace{\vphantom{\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in I_{t+1}}a_{i}}\cdots}_{\in\mathcal{A}_{1}})=0

by freeness of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
from
\mathcal{A}_{2}
(it is
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
applied to an alternated product of variables which are centered variables with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
, except possibly the first and the last one).
∎

2.3.
Infinitesimal free cumulants

In the course of building cumulants for infinitesimal freeness (see Definition
2.1.1
), we will distinguish two complementary cases:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=0
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})\neq 0
. In
[
14
]
, the authors tackled the first case; we briefly recall how their cumulants are defined and move on to treat the second case.

Given a sequence of linear maps
f_{n}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C}\penalty\ ,n\geq 1
and second sequence of linear maps
\partial f_{n}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C},\penalty\ n\geq 1
, we combine them to obtain a third family of linear maps indexed by non-crossing partitions. The heuristic to this definition is applying the Leibniz rule to (
3
). Given a non-crossing partition
\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)
,

(8)

\partial f_{\pi}(a_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\pi\\
V=(j_{1}<\ldots<j_{k})\end{subarray}}\partial f_{k}(a_{j_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{j_{k}})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\neq W\in\pi\\
W=(i_{1}<\ldots<i_{\ell})\end{subarray}}f_{\ell}(a_{i_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{i_{\ell}}).

The notation
\partial
is due to Mingo and allows deriving relations by formal derivations. For example, if
\pi_{1}\in{\rm{\rm NC}}(\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
\pi_{2}\in{\rm{\rm NC}}(\{m+1,\ldots,m+n\}
, for any
\vec{a}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes m}
and
\vec{b}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}
, we have

(9)

\partial f_{\pi_{1}\cup\pi_{2}}(\vec{a},\vec{b})=\partial f_{\pi_{1}}(\vec{a})\cdot f_{\pi_{2}}(\vec{b})+f_{\pi_{1}}(\vec{a})\cdot\partial f_{\pi_{2}}(\vec{b}).

When
\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=0
, the definition of infinitesimal free cumulants of a pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
follows by taking the formal derivative
\partial
of
(
4
)
.

2.3.1 Definition
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital complex algebra equipped with two linear functionals
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
where
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is unital
and

\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=0
. The
infinitesimal free cumulants

(\partial\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C})_{n\geq 1}

are defined from
\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
and
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
by the following inductive definition, for any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
:

(10)

\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=\partial\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{n}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{\pi\neq{1}_{n}\in{\rm NC}(n)}\partial\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}).

2.3.2 Theorem

(Theorem 4.1.2 of
[
14
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital complex algebra equipped with two linear functionals
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
where
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=1
, and
\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=0
.
Infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
is equivalent to the vanishing of mixed free cumulants
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
and of mixed infinitesimal free cumulants
(\partial\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
.

The condition
\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=0
is important in the theorem above. In order to characterize infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})\neq 0
, a possible solution is to translate
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
by
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
: infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
yields infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1)\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
, and the converse is also true. As a consequence, the infinitesimal cumulants of the couple
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1)\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
characterize infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
.

We follow another, quite more intricate road, but epitomic of the method we will use in the sequel. We demonstrate it now. It consists first in finding some unital companion to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
, denoted by
\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners
, such that freeness with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
implies infinitesimal freeness with respect to the couple
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners)
. Defining
\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners
requires going to the envelope
T(\mathcal{A})
of
\mathcal{A}
; this is the main drawback. Then, infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
is equivalent to infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
where
\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1)\cdot\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners
. Finally, infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
is equivalent to the vanishing of the mixed free cumulants
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
and of the mixed infinitesimally free cumulants
(\partial\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
defined from
\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})\cdot\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners
by (
10
).

We define the unital linear functional
\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners:T(\mathcal{A})\to\mathbb{C}
by, for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
:

(11)

\displaystyle\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{l}\leq n}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{i_{k}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i_{k+1}-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\rm NC}(\llbracket n\rrbracket\backslash\llbracket i_{1},i_{l}\rrbracket)\\
i_{1}-1\sim_{\pi}i_{l}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{i_{1}-1}\otimes a_{i_{l}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}),

\displaystyle\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners(1_{T(\mathcal{A})})=1.

We recall that
i_{1}-1\sim_{\pi}i_{l}
means that
i_{1}-1
and
i_{l}
are two integers in the same block in the partition
\pi
. After proposition
2.4.5
, we define another functional denoted with the symbol
[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}]
which coincides with
\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners
only in the case where
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is tracial.
Boolean cumulants are expressed from free cumulants as a sum over
irreducible
non-crossing partitions
[
3
]
(non-crossing partitions for which
1
and
n
are in the same blocks), for any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
:

(12)

\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\sum_{\pi\in{\rm NC}_{\rm irr}(n)}\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}).

The right hand-side of (
11
) can be written as a double sum ranging over non-crossing partitions and tuples
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{l}
such that
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\}
is a block in the Kreweras complement of
\pi
:

(13)

\displaystyle\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{l}\leq n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)\\
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\}\in{\rm Kr}(\pi)\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)\end{subarray}}(n+1-|\pi|)\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{\pi}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}).

We extend
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
from
\mathcal{A}
to
T(\mathcal{A})
(and use the same symbol for the resulting maps) by using the formula:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n}),\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n}).

for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
.

2.3.3 Proposition
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
be anon-commutative probability space and suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are two
free sub-algebras
of
\mathcal{A}
. Then
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})
and
T(\mathcal{A}_{2})
are two
infinitesimally free
sub-algebras of
T(\mathcal{A})
with respect to the pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners)
.

Proof.

We pick a centered alternated sequence
\vec{x}_{1},\ldots,\vec{x}_{n}
, with
\vec{x}_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{i}}
,
j_{i}\in\{1,2\}
. We have to show that

\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners(\vec{x}_{1}\cdots\vec{x}_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(\vec{x}_{1}\cdots\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners(\vec{x}_{i})\cdots\vec{x}_{n}).

We write
\vec{x}_{1}\cdots\vec{x_{n}}=a_{1}\cdots a_{N}
, where
\vec{x}_{j}=a_{l_{j}}\cdots a_{l_{j+1}-1}
,
1=l_{1}<\cdots<l_{n+1}=N+1
.
Now, pick
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{l}\leq N
. Since the
\vec{x}_{i}
are mutually free and by expanding the Boolean cumulants as a sum over irreducible partitions of free cumulants, for
\beta(a_{i_{k}},\ldots,a_{i_{k+1}-1})
to be different from zero, it is necessary that
a_{i_{k}}
and
a_{i_{k+1}-1}
belongs to the same algebra, say
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

Suppose that
i_{k}
and
i_{k+1}
are such that
i_{k}<l_{j+1}<l_{j+2}\leq i_{k+1}
; there exists a words
\vec{x}_{j+1}
on variables in
\mathcal{A}_{2}
comprised in between the two letters
a_{i_{k}}
and
a_{i_{k+1}-1}
.
We write

\beta(a_{i_{k}},\ldots,a_{i_{k+1}-1})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\subset\llbracket i_{k},i_{k+1}-1\rrbracket,\\
\pi\in{\rm{\rm NC}}(\llbracket i_{k},i_{k+1}-1\rrbracket),\\
V\in\pi\end{subarray}}\kappa_{V}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{V})\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{\tilde{\pi}}(a_{\llbracket i_{k},i_{k+1}-1\rrbracket\backslash V}),

where in the above sum,
\tilde{\pi}=\pi\backslash V
. Performing first the summation over
\tilde{\pi}
yields

\sum_{\tilde{\pi}\in{\rm{\rm NC}}(\llbracket i_{k},i_{k+1}-1\rrbracket\backslash V}\kappa_{V}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{V})\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{\tilde{\pi}}(a_{\llbracket i_{k},i_{k+1}-1\rrbracket\backslash V})={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}_{I_{V}}(a_{\llbracket i_{k},i_{k+1}-1\rrbracket\backslash V}),

where
I_{V}
is the maximal interval partition contained in
{\llbracket i_{k},i_{k+1}-1\rrbracket\backslash V}
. Now, there is a block
W\in I_{V}
which corresponding term in the product
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}_{I_{V}}(a_{\llbracket i_{k},i_{k+1}-1\rrbracket\backslash V})
reads

(14)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(\vec{y}\cdots\vec{x}_{\ell+1}\cdots\vec{z}),

where
\vec{y}\cdots\vec{x}_{\ell+1}\cdots\vec{z}
is an alternated word with entries in
\mathbb{C}\langle x_{a},a\in\mathcal{A}\rangle
, all centered with possibly the exception of the two edging letters
\vec{y}
and
\vec{z}
. We apply freeness to infer the that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(\vec{y}\cdots\vec{x}_{\ell+1}\cdots\vec{z})=0
.

We next show that the sum over the
i^{\prime}s
reduces to the sum with same general term but ranging over the
i^{\prime}s
that belong to the same interval
\llbracket\ell_{j},\ell_{j+1}-1\rrbracket
. The complementary sum to the latter ranges over the
i^{\prime}s
for which there exist two entries
i_{k}<i_{l}
, with
i_{k}<l_{j}<l_{j+1}<i_{l}
. We perform the partial sum of those
i^{\prime}s
which contain
i_{k}
and
i_{l}
. From the previous paragraph, the general term is a product with a term equal to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(\vec{x}_{j})=0
since

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(\vec{x}_{j})=\sum_{l_{j}\leq i_{b}<i_{b+1}<\cdots i_{b+x}\leq l_{j+1}}\prod_{s=1}^{x-1}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{i_{b+s}},\ldots,a_{i_{b+s+1}-1}).

It follows that the entire complementary sum is equal to
0
. We have proven the following equality, under the notations introduced so far:

(15)

\displaystyle\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners(\vec{x}_{1}\cdots\vec{x}_{n})=\sum_{a=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell_{a}\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{l}\leq\ell_{a+1}-1}

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{i_{k}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i_{k+1}-1})

(16)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\rm{\rm NC}}([n]\backslash\llbracket i_{1},i_{l}\rrbracket)\\
i_{1}-1\sim_{\pi}i_{l}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{i_{1}-1}\otimes a_{i_{l}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}).

It remains to study the factorization of the sum on the second line in the above equation. Pick a non-crossing partition
\pi\in{\rm NC}([n]\backslash\llbracket i_{1}-1,i_{l}\rrbracket)
such that
i_{1}-1\sim_{\pi}i_{l}
and call
V
the block that contains those two integers. We want to prove that this block is contained in the interval
\llbracket l_{a},l_{a+1}-1\rrbracket
. Assuming it is not the case, it implies that there exists a third integer
v\in V
, such that
i_{l}<v
. Doing the partial summation over non-crossing partitions
\tilde{\pi}\in{\rm NC}([n]\backslash\llbracket i_{1}-1,i_{l}\rrbracket)
that contains
V
results in a term equal to a product, one term of which has the form (
14
). It is thus equal to
0
. We conclude that

(17)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\rm{\rm NC}}([n]\backslash\llbracket i_{1},i_{l}\rrbracket)\\
i_{1}-1\sim_{\pi}i_{l}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{i_{1}-1}\otimes a_{i_{l}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\rm{\rm NC}}(\llbracket l_{a},l_{a+1}\llbracket\backslash\llbracket i_{1},i_{l}\rrbracket)\\
i_{1}-1\sim_{\pi}i_{l}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{\llbracket l_{a},l_{a+1}\llbracket}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{[n]\backslash\llbracket l_{a},l_{a+1}\llbracket}).

The result follows by combining (
15
) and (
17
).
∎

2.3.4 Definition
.

The infinitesimal cumulants with respect to the couple
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
are the restriction to
T(\mathcal{A})
of the infinitesimal cumulants
\partial\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}
of
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
defined from
\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})\cdot\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners
by (
10
). More precisely, the following moments-cumulants formula, for any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
, are in hold:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1)\cdot\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)}\partial\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}).

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1)\cdot\sum_{\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)}(n+1-|\pi|)\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)}\partial\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}).

2.3.5 Example
.

The infinisimal cumulants of
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
are given by, for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},\penalty\ n\geq 1
:

\partial\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{n}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=(1-n)\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{n}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}).

2.3.6 Theorem
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital complex algebra equipped with two linear functionals
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
where
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is unital. Cyclic freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
is equivalent to the vanishing of the mixed free cumulants
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
and of the mixed infinitesimally free cumulants
(\partial\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
constructed from
\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})\cdot\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners
.

Proof.

Infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
in
\mathcal{A}
is equivalent to infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
in
T(\mathcal{A})
. But infinitesimal freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
in
T(\mathcal{A})
is equivalent to cyclic freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1)\cdot\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners)
(by Proposition
2.3.3
), which is equivalent to to the vanishing of mixed free cumulants
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
and of mixed infinitesimal cumulants
(\partial\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
(by Theorem
2.3.2
).
∎

2.3.7 Remark
.

As a final note to this section, we remark that the formula (
13
) that defines the linear functional
\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners
was independently proposed by
[
16
]
as a definition for the multi-variate inverse Markov-Krein transform. The motivation of the authors for such a formula was to compute the fluctuations between the non-commutative distribution of unitarily invariant random Hermitian matrices and their principal submatrices. In Proposition
3.2.1
, we have another definition for the multi-variate inverse Markov-Krein transform, denoted with the symbol
[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}]
, and which coincide with
\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners
only in the case where
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is tracial.

2.4.
Cyclic-free cumulants

We now address the problem of defining cumulants for cyclic freeness (see Definition
3.1.2
). We follow the same method as exposed in the previous section: we begin by defining these cumulants when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
is trivial on the unit
{1}_{\mathcal{A}}
. In a second time, we exhibit a pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}])
such that freeness with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
implies cylic-freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}])
. Finally we define the cyclic free cumulants of
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
as the cyclic free cumulants of
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
where
D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})\cdot[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}]
.

Given a sequence of
cyclically invariant
linear maps
Df_{n}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C}
,

Df_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})=Df_{n}(a_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{i}),\quad a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A},\penalty\ \penalty\ i\in[n],\penalty\ \penalty\ n\geq 1,

the previous definition (
8
) of the associated functionals indexed by non-crossing partitions is not adequate, if we want to impose cyclic invariance in the following sense:

Df_{\pi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=Df_{c\cdot\pi}(a_{c(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{c(n)}),\penalty\ a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A},

where
c=(1\cdots n)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
c\cdot\pi
is given by

c\cdot\pi:=\Big\{\{i+1:i\in V\},\penalty\ V\in\pi\Big\}
(with
n+1=1
).
Defining these maps by using (
31
) does not yield cyclically invariant maps. In fact, there is a unique family of linear maps
(Df_{\pi}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C}:{\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)})
from
(Df_{n})_{n\geq 1}
with the required invariance and satisfying the Leibniz formula:

\scriptstyle\bullet

Df_{1_{n}}=Df_{n}

\scriptstyle\bullet

for non-crossing partitions
\pi_{1}\in{\rm NC}(\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
\pi_{2}\in{\rm NC}(\{m+1,\ldots,m+n\}
and for any
{a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}
,
{b_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{m}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes m}
:

\displaystyle Df_{\pi_{1}\cup\pi_{2}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\otimes b_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{m})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=Df_{\pi_{1}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})\cdot f_{\pi_{2}}(b_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{m})+f_{\pi_{1}}({a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}})\cdot Df_{\pi_{2}}({b_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{m}}).

2.4.1 Example
.

Df_{\{1,4\},\{2,3\}}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})=Df_{\{1,2\},\{3,4\}}(a_{2},a_{3},a_{4},a_{1})=f_{2}(a_{2},a_{3})Df_{2}(a_{4},a_{1})+Df_{2}(a_{2},a_{3})f_{2}(a_{4},a_{1}).

The
Df_{\pi},\penalty\ \pi\in{\rm NC}
satisfy to equation (
8
) but at the price of reordering the
a^{\prime}s
. Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
\pi
be a non-crossing partition of the cyclically ordered set
\llbracket n\rrbracket
(endowed with the canonical cylic order
\prec
). A block
V\in\pi
yields a partial order
<_{V}
on the union of all the other blocks of
\pi
:

a,b\in\bigcup_{W\neq V\in\pi}W\quad a<_{V}b\Leftrightarrow\penalty\ v_{i}\prec a\prec b\prec v_{i+1}

if
V=\{v_{1}<\cdots<v_{k}\}
with the convention
v_{k+1}=v_{1}
. Each cyclic interval
\rrbracket v_{i},v_{i+1}\llbracket_{\prec}
is a chain for the order
<_{V}
.

Note that the ordering
<_{V}

can be different from the restriction to

\bigcup_{W\neq V\in\pi}W

of the natural ordering on

\llbracket n\rrbracket
(it does, in fact, each time
v_{1}\neq 1
and
v_{k}\neq n
).
The following proposition is obvious.

2.4.2 Proposition
.

Given
\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
, the following formula is in hold:

Df_{\pi}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\pi\\
V=\{j_{1}<\ldots<j_{k}\}\end{subarray}}Df_{k}(a_{j_{1}},\ldots,a_{j_{k}})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}W\in\pi,W\neq V\\
W=\{i_{1}<_{V}\ldots<_{V}i_{\ell}\}\end{subarray}}f_{\ell}(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{\ell}}).

Figure 3.
We consider the partition
\pi=\{\{\{1\},\{2,6,15\},\{3,5\},\{4\},\{7,11,12\},\{8,10\},\{9\}\{13,14\},\{16\}\}\}
. We draw the pair
(\pi,V)
with
V
a block of
\pi
as follows: we draw two concentric circumferences (one in red and the other in black), we connect the legs of the block
V
to inner circumference and draw the order blocks as usual.

At this point, it is crucial to notice that we have two ways at our disposal to define multilinear maps indexed by non-crossing partitions from a pair of sequences of multilinear maps on the base algebra. The first way is exposed in the context of infinitesimal free independence, and the second one is presented in the paragraph just above about cyclic freeness. Both ways yield maps satisfying some sort of Leibniz relations. We adopt the redundant notation
{\rm CyNC}
for the set of non-crossing partitions to emphasize that in

Df_{\pi},\quad\pi\in{\rm CyNC},

the
Df_{\pi}
satisfy the equation
in Proposition (
2.4.2
). In the above formula (
2.4.2
), we may write:

f_{\ell}(a_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i_{\ell}})=f_{\ell}(a_{(W,<_{V})})

with the convention that
a_{(W,<_{V})}
means that we restrict the letters of
a
to those with an index in
W
and they are read according to
<_{V}
.

Given a partition
\pi\in{\rm CyNC}(n)
, one defines another ensemble of partial orders on
\llbracket n\rrbracket
. We pick a block in the Kreweras complement of
\pi
, and consider
<_{R}
, defined over the complementary set of
R
in
\{1,1^{\prime},\ldots,n,n^{\prime}\}
restricted to
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Finally, we remark that the right side of (
2.4.2
) makes sense even in the case where
Df_{n}
is not tracial. This will be used in the following proposition.
In the case where
D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=0
, the definition of cyclic free cumulants follows by applying formally
D
to
(
4
)
.
A word of caution is in order, notation-wise. The notation
Df_{\pi}
does not record the linear maps
f
. Whenever there is ambiguity, we adopt the more explicit notation
[f,Df]_{\pi}
. We will extensively use this notation in Section
4.2
.

2.4.3 Definition

(Cyclic free cumulants)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital complex algebra equipped with a linear functionals
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
where
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is unital and
D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is tracial with
D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=0
. The
cyclic free cumulants
of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is a pair:

(D\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C})_{n\geq 1},\quad(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathcal{A})_{n\geq 1}

defined inductively by:

(18)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{1_{n}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{1_{n}\neq\pi\in{\rm CyNC}(n)}\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}),

(19)

\displaystyle D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{1_{n}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{\pi\neq 1_{n}\in{\rm CyNC}(n)}D\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}).

2.4.4 Example
.

Here are example of computations, we have colored in red the terms that differ from the infinitesimal free moments-cumulants relations:

\displaystyle D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1})=D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}),

\displaystyle D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}a_{2})=D\kappa_{2}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})+D\kappa_{1}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{2})+{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1})D\kappa_{1}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2}),

\displaystyle D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})=D\kappa_{2}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{3})+{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1})D\kappa_{2}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2}\otimes a_{3})+{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{2})D\kappa_{2}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2}\otimes a_{3})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{2}\otimes a_{3})+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{3}\otimes a_{1})}+D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{3}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}a_{2}).

At this point it is not clear whether
D\kappa^{\psi}
as defined above is cyclically invariant. This will be implied by cyclic invariant of
(D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{1_{n}})_{n\geq 1}
which we state in the next proposition.

2.4.5 Proposition
.

The cyclic free cumulants
(D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{1_{n}})_{n\geq 1}
are cyclically invariant, for any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}
:

D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{c(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{c(n)}).

Proof.

It a simple consequence of the fact that the
D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{1_{n}}
,
n\geq 1
are uniquely determined by the equation (
18
) and that
(D\kappa_{1_{n}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}\circ c)_{n\geq 1}
where
c
is the cyclic permutations of the arguments satisfies (
18
).
∎

2.4.6 Theorem
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital complex algebra equipped with two linear functionals
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
where
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is unital and
D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is tracial with
D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=0
.
Cyclic freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
is equivalent to the vanishing of mixed free cumulants
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
and of mixed cyclic free cumulants
(D\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
.

Proof.

See the following Section
2.5
.
∎

We now turn to the general case
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})\neq 0
. We start with the definition of the unital map
[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}]:T(\mathcal{A})\to\mathbb{C}
:

(20)

\displaystyle[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}](a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{l}\leq n}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{i_{k}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i_{k+1}-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{i_{l}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i_{1}-1}).

Note that
[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}]
coincides with
\lcorners{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}\rcorners
when
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
is tracial.

A cyclic interval partition
I\neq\mathbb{1}_{n}
of
[n]
has a Kreweras complement which contain only a single non-trivial (not a singleton) block. We write
\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{I}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})
for
\prod_{V\in I}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{(V,\prec_{R})})
where
R
is the only non-trivial block in the Kreweras complement of
I
. With these definitions, for any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}](a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{n}(a_{i}\otimes\cdots a_{n}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i-1})+\sum_{I\neq\mathbb{1}_{n}\in{\rm CyInt}(n)}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{I}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}).

In Section
3.4
, we will interpret this formula as some Boolean cyclic moments-cumulants formula, definion
[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}]
via its cyclic Boolean cumulants given by averaging the ordinary Boolean cumulants of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
. Notice that the sum over the blocks of the Kreweras complement in the right-hand side of the above formula is not always equal to
(n+1-|\pi|)\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{\pi}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})
if
\psi
is not tracial.
It is possible to give a formula for
[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}]
in terms of the free cumulants of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
:

\displaystyle[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}](a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=\sum_{\pi\in{\rm NC}(n)}\sum_{R\in{\rm Kr}(\pi)}\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{\pi}(a_{(\llbracket n\rrbracket,<_{R})}).

2.4.7 Theorem
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an unital algebra and
\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
two free unital algebra with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
. Then, the two algebras
T(\mathcal{A}_{1})
and
T(\mathcal{A}_{2})
are cylically free with respect to the pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}])
.

Proof.

We postpone the proof to Section
3.2
, where a more general result is proved. See Corollary
3.2.3
.
∎

We now define the cyclic free cumulants for a general pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
as the restriction of cyclic free cumulants to
T(\mathcal{A})
(see Definition
2.4.3
) of the pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}])
.

2.4.8 Definition

(Cyclic-free cumulants – general case)
.

The cyclic free cumulants with respect to the couple
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
are the restriction to
T(\mathcal{A})
of the cyclic free cumulants
D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}
of
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
defined from
D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})\cdot[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}]
ny Definition
2.4.3
). More precisely, the following moments-cumulants formula, for any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
, are in hold:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})\cdot[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}](a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\rm CyNC}\end{subarray}}D\kappa_{\pi}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}).

2.4.9 Example
.

With
\delta={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})
, we have

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1})=\delta\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1})+D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}),

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}a_{2})=\delta(\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})+\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1})\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2}))+D\kappa_{2}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})+D\kappa_{1}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{2})+{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1})D\kappa_{1}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2}),

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})=\delta\{\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes a_{3})+\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{3})+\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{3})\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2}\otimes a_{3})\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1})+\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1})\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2})\beta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{3})\}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+D\kappa_{2}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}\otimes a_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{3})+{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1})D\kappa_{2}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2}\otimes a_{3})+{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{2})D\kappa_{2}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2}\otimes a_{3})

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{1}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{2}\otimes a_{3})+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{3}\otimes a_{1})}+D\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(a_{3}){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a_{1}a_{2}).

We now state the main Theorem of this section.

2.4.10 Theorem
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital complex algebra equipped with two linear functionals
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
tracial. Then, cyclic freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
is equivalent to the vanishing of mixed free cumulants
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
and of mixed cyclic free cumulants
(D\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
.

Proof.

Cyclic freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
in
\mathcal{A}
is equivalent to cyclic freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
in
T(\mathcal{A})
. But cyclic freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
in
T(\mathcal{A})
is equivalent to cyclic freeness with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-\delta[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}])
(by Proposition
2.1.6
), which is equivalent to to the vanishing of mixed free cumulants
(\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
and of mixed cyclic free cumulants
(D\kappa_{n}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})_{n\geq 1}
(by Theorem
2.4.6
).
∎

2.5.
Proof of Theorem
2.4.6

The objective of this section is to apply the method proposed by F. Lehner in
[
21
]
based on the Good’s formula for classical cumulants. Actually, we are in a setting that slightly outflanks the one of
[
21
]
, since we do not assume our functionals to be unital. For the convenience of the reader, we outline the main steps of the procedure.

Pick
n\geq 1
an integer. As a quick reminder, Good’s formula for the
n
-th
classical cumulants

c_{n}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
of a sequence of (essentially bounded) random variables
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
states, for any integer
n\geq 1
,

c_{n}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}^{[n]}_{1}\cdots\tilde{X}^{[n]}_{n}]

where the random variables
\tilde{X}^{[n]}_{i},\penalty\ 1\leq i\leq n
are defined by

\tilde{X}^{[n]}_{i}=\xi X_{i}+\xi^{2}X^{(2)}_{i}+\cdots+\xi^{n}X_{i}^{(n)},\quad\xi=e^{\frac{2i\pi}{n}}

and
(X_{i}^{(k)})_{1\leq k,i\leq n}
is a square array of independent random variables with
X_{i}^{(k)}=_{\rm dist.}X_{i}
,
1\leq k\leq n
. The
partitioned cumulants
satisfies a similar formula. Let
\pi
be a partition of
[n]
and denote by
c_{\pi}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
the corresponding cumulants defined by:

c_{\pi}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}):=\prod_{B=\{i_{1}<\ldots<i_{p}\}\in\pi}c_{|B|}(X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{p}}).

For each block
B
of
\pi
and for any
i\in B
, we pick
X_{i}^{(k)},\penalty\ 1\leq k\leq|B|
independent copies distributed as
X_{i}
and set:

\tilde{X}^{B}_{i}:=\xi_{|B|}X_{i}^{(1)}+\xi_{|B|}^{2}X_{i}^{(2)}+\cdots+\xi_{|B|}^{|B|}X_{i}^{(|B|)}

where
\xi_{|B|}
is a
|B|
-th root of the unit and, one has

c_{\pi}(X_{1}\ldots,X_{n})=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}_{1}^{B_{(1)}}\cdots\tilde{X}_{n}^{B_{(n)}}]

where for each
