\omega_{x}(z)=z\rho_{x}(z\rho_{y}(\omega_{x}(z))),\quad\omega_{y}(z)=z\rho_{y}(z\rho_{x}(\omega_{y}(z))),

with the
\rho
-series
\rho_{x}
of a variable
x\in\mathcal{A}
defined as:

\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z).

They satisfy the following properties which allow numerical computations of the distributions of
xy
when
x
and
y
are free:

\displaystyle\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)=\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)=\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z),\quad\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)=z\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z).

For Boolean multiplicative convolution, the appropriate transform to consider is the
\rho
-transform, which is a shift of the
\eta
-transform, see
[
28
]
:

(35)

\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)=\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)

whenever
x-1
is Boolean independent of
y-1
in a probability space
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
.
For monotone convolution, the following relation holds, whenever
x-1
is independent from
y
, see
[
27
]
:

\displaystyle\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)=\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}\circ\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z).

The next proposition can be found in
[
4
]
in the context of additive convolution of measures on the positive real line or on the torus, but we provide a proof here though for the convenience of the reader.

4.2.2 Proposition
.

Let
x,y
be infinitesimally free random variables in an infinitesimal probability space
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}^{\prime})
, then:

\displaystyle\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{xy}(z)=\Big(z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm ln}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z))\Big)(\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{x}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z))+\Big(z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm ln}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z))\Big)(\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)),

or else:

\displaystyle\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\rho_{xy}(z)=\Big(\frac{d}{dz}\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)\Big)(\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\rho_{x}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z))+\Big(\frac{d}{dz}\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)\Big)(\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\rho_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)).

Proof.

One might first consider the case where both
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(x)
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(y)
are invertible.Then, from the definition of
\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}S_{x}
, one gets:

-\frac{d}{dz}M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)(\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}S_{x}\circ M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z))=\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}M_{x}(z).

The above formula yields:

\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{x}(z)=\frac{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}M_{x}(z)}{(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)+1)^{2}}=-\frac{\frac{d}{dz}M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)}{(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)+1)^{2}}(\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}S_{x}\circ M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z))=K_{x}(z)(\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}S_{x}(z)\circ M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)).

where we have set:

K_{x}(z)=-\frac{\frac{d}{dz}M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)}{(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)+1)^{2}}

From the following formula for the infinitesimal
S
-transform
\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}S_{xy}
of
xy
:

\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}S_{xy}(z)=\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}S_{x}(z)S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)+S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}S_{y}(z).

we infer, again for
z
small enough:

\frac{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}((M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}_{xy}(z))}{K_{xy}\circ(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}_{xy}(z)}=\frac{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}((M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}_{x}(z))}{K_{x}\circ(M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)}S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)+S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)\frac{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}((M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z))}{K_{y}\circ(M_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)}

and:

\frac{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}{\eta}_{xy}(z)}{K_{xy}(z)}=\frac{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{x}((M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x})^{\langle-1\rangle}\circ M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))}{K_{x}((M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x})^{\langle-1\rangle}\circ M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))}S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))+S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))\frac{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{y}((M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y})^{\langle-1\rangle}\circ M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))}{K_{y}((M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y})^{\langle-1\rangle}\circ M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))}

In the case where the multiplicative subordination series
[
5
]
:

\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)=({\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}}^{\langle-1\rangle}\circ\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy})(z),\quad({\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}}^{\langle-1\rangle}\circ\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy})(z).

One obtains, from equation (
4.2
):

\frac{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)}{K_{xy}(z)}=S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))\frac{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}\circ\omega_{x}(z)}{K_{x}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)}+S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))\frac{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)}{K_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)}.

We compute the right-hand side of this last equation as follows:

\frac{K_{xy}(z)}{K_{x}\circ\omega_{x}(z)}=\frac{M_{xy}^{\prime}(z)}{(M_{xy}(z)+1)^{2}}[\frac{M_{x}^{\prime}(\omega_{x}(z))}{(M_{x}\circ\omega_{x}+1)^{2}}]^{-1}=\omega^{\prime}_{x}(z).

This yields:

\displaystyle{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)}

\displaystyle=S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))\frac{K_{xy}(z)}{K_{x}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)}\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{x}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)+S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))\frac{K_{xy}(z)}{K_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)}\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)

\displaystyle=S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))\frac{d}{dz}\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{x}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)+S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))\frac{d}{dz}\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z).

We multiply both side of the equality by
\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)
:

{\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)}=[\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)S_{y}(M_{xy}(z))]\frac{d}{dz}\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)\partial\eta_{x}\circ\omega_{x}(z)+[\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)S_{x}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))]\frac{d}{dz}\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)\partial\eta_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)

Now,

\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z))=\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)\frac{1+M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)}{M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)}(M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y})^{\langle-1\rangle}\circ M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)=\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z).

Thus:

\displaystyle\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)\partial\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)=\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)\{\frac{d}{dz}\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)\partial\eta_{x}\circ\omega_{x}(z)\}+\omega_{x}(z)\{\frac{d}{dz}\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)\partial\eta_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)\}

We drop the assumptions
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(x)
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(y)
invertible. Then, when
\varepsilon>0
is small,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(x+\varepsilon)=\varepsilon={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(y+\varepsilon)
is different from zero. As formal series,
\eta_{x+\varepsilon}\rightarrow\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}
and
\eta_{y+\varepsilon}\to\eta_{y}
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. We argue that
\omega_{x+\varepsilon}
and
\omega_{y+\varepsilon}
converge too.
Recall, from
[
22
]
,

\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x+\varepsilon}(z)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{2n+1}(x+\varepsilon,y+\varepsilon,\ldots,y+\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)z^{n+1}\to\sum_{n\geq 0}\beta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{2n+1}(x,y,\ldots,y,x)z^{n+1}

as formal series, since
x+\varepsilon 1_{\mathcal{A}}
converges in distribution to
x
as
\varepsilon
goes to
0
.
∎

We move on to prove a subordination formula for the
\eta
-transform of the product of two conditionally free random variables. The formula we obtain can be easily guessed from the rule of thumb that such formula is inferred from the subordination formula for the additive convolution (see equation (
34
)) upon replacing the
F
-transform by the
\eta
-transform (and the addition of series by the Cauchy product of series).

4.2.3 Proposition
.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{A}
be conditionally free random variables in a conditional probability space
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
, then the following equality of formal series holds:

\displaystyle\eta_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)\eta_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z)=\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))\eta_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)),

or else:

(36)

\rho_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z)=\rho_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))\rho_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)).

Proof.

We refer to
[
30
]
for the definition of the various transforms we will use. As before, we will first treat the case where
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(x)\neq 0
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(y)\neq 0
. In particular we will be using the conditional
R
-transform
C^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}\in\mathbb{C}[[z]]
, defined by the following functional equation:

\displaystyle C_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}[z(1+M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z))](1+M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z))=M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)(1+M^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)).

The conditional cumulants
\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{n}(x)
of
x
are defined as the Taylor coefficients at
0
of the (shifted) conditional
R
-tranform:

C_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\kappa^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{n}(x)z^{n}.

From the previous equation
\tilde{C}_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z):=\frac{1}{z}C^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)
is well-defined as a series in
\mathbb{C}[[z]]
. The conditional
S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}
transform of
x
is defined by:

\displaystyle(S_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z))^{-1}:=\tilde{C}_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}[(C^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)].

Note that
(C^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z))^{\langle-1\rangle}
is well-defined since
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(x)\neq 0
.
Recall that simple arithmetic manipulations yield:

M^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(\frac{z}{1+C^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)})=C^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)\Leftrightarrow M^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(w)=C_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(w(1+M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(w))),

and further:

w\tilde{C}_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(w)(1+M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\frac{w}{1+C_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(w)}))=M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(\frac{w}{1+C_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(w)})(1+C_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(w))).

Substituting
w=(C_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)=(1+z)(M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)
in the previous formula yields:

(M^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)\tilde{C}_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}[(C^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)]=\frac{M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}[(M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)]}{1+M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}[(M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)]}.

We thus obtain for the conditional
S
-transform of
x
:

S_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)=(1+M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}[(M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)])\frac{(M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)}{M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}[(M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)]}=(\frac{z}{\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)})\circ(M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}(z).

Since the conditional
S
-transform is multiplicative over conditionally free multiplicative convolution
[
30
]
:

S_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)=S^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)S_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}|{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)

implies:

\frac{z}{\eta_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z)}=[\frac{z}{\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)}][(M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}\circ(M_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})(z)][\frac{z}{\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)}][(M_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\langle-1\rangle}\circ(M_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})(z)].

Hence:

\eta_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z)=\frac{z}{\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)).

This concludes the proof since
\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)=z\eta_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)
.
∎

4.2.4 Remark
.

We will use the notations introduced by the author in
[
23
]
. Given two distributions
\mu_{x},\mu_{y}\colon\mathbb{C}\langle x\rangle\to\mathbb{C}
, their multiplicative monotone convolution, denoted by
\mu_{x}\circlearrowright\mu_{y}
is defined through the following relation on the
\eta
-transforms:

\eta_{\mu_{x}\circlearrowright\mu_{y}}=\eta_{\mu_{x}}\circ\eta_{\mu_{y}}.

The subordination series
\omega_{\mu_{x}}
coincides with the
\eta
-series of the s-free convolution
\mu_{x}\boxslash\mu_{y}
of the two distributions
\mu_{x}
and
\mu_{y}
.
The Boolean convolution
\mu_{x}\boxasterisk\mu_{y}
of the two distributions
\mu_{x}
and
\mu_{y}
is the distribution whose
\rho
-transform satisfies:

\rho_{\mu_{x}\boxasterisk\mu_{y}}=\rho_{\mu_{x}}\rho_{\mu_{y}},

The statement of the previous proposition
4.2.3
can be restated as follows:

\mu^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}\prescript{}{\mu_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}}{\boxtimes}{}_{\mu_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}}\mu^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}=(\mu^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}\angle\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}\boxslash\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x})\boxasterisk(\mu^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}\angle\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}\boxslash\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}),

where
\angle
is the
orthogonal convolution of distributions

[
23
]
, by definition:

\rho_{\mu_{x}\angle\mu_{y}}(z)=\rho_{\mu_{x}}\circ\eta_{\mu_{y}}(z).

and recall that the s-free convolution is the solution to the fixed point equation
\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}\angle\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}\boxslash\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}=\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}\boxslash\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}
and

\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}\boxtimes\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}=(\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}\boxslash\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x})\boxasterisk(\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}\boxslash\mu^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}).

4.2.5 Remark
.

The previous proposition is a statement on formal series. To promote the equality (
36
) to an equality between holomorphic functions on the upper-half plane, one has to require
x,y
to be positive operators or unitaries. In that case, the subordination series
\omega_{x}
and
\omega_{y}
are the expansion around the origin of anayltic functions defiend over the complex minus the positive half-line.

4.2.6 Remark
.

The motivation of the authors in
[
13
]
was to give a combinatorial interpretation and a multivariate extension of the result contained in Proposition
[
4
]
elucidating a link between conditional additive convolution and infinitesimal additive convolution (in a nutshell : taking distributional derivative intertwines conditionally free additive convolution and infinitesimal convolution). In particular,

G^{W({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})}_{x}(z)=\frac{d}{dz}h_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z),\quad h_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z):=\rho_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\frac{1}{z}).

In view of the last two propositions, it is natural to ask whether there exists some linear functional
{\sf\tilde{W}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
on
T(\mathcal{A})
such that:

\frac{d}{dz}\ln(\rho_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z))=\partial^{{\sf\tilde{W}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})}\rho_{x}(z)=\frac{M_{x}^{{\sf\tilde{W}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})}}{(1+M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{2}}.

For in that case, we would have, if
x
and
y
are infinitisimally free in
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\sf\tilde{W}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}))
and conditionally free in
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})

\partial^{{\sf\tilde{W}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})}\rho_{xy}(z)=\frac{d}{dz}{\rm ln}(\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{xy}(z)).

Taking
{\sf\tilde{W}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})={\sf{W}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
does not work.

For readability, we set
\delta=1-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})
in the next proposition.

4.2.7 Proposition
.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{A}
be two cyclic c-free random variables in a cyclic-conditional probability space
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
,

\displaystyle{M}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{xy}(z)=z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))

\displaystyle{M}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{y}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))+z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)){M}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{x}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z))-z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(\frac{1-\eta_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z)}{(1-\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))(1-\eta_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z))})

\displaystyle\hskip 241.84842pt-\delta z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(1-\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{xy}(z))

or else:

\displaystyle\tilde{M}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{xy}(z)=\frac{d}{dz}\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)

\displaystyle\tilde{M}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{y}(\omega_{x}(z))+\frac{d}{dz}\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)\tilde{M}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{x}(\omega_{y}(z))-\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(\frac{1-z\rho_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z)}{(1-\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)\rho_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))(1-\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)\rho_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z))})

\displaystyle\hskip 241.84842pt-\delta\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(1-\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{xy}(z)).

Proof.

We compute first:

\displaystyle\partial^{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}]}\eta_{x}(z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\partial^{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}]}M_{x}(z)}{(M^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=(1-\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z))^{2}(\partial^{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}]}M_{x}(z))

\displaystyle=(1-\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z))^{2}(z\frac{\frac{d}{dz}(M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})(z)}{1+M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z)})

\displaystyle=(1-\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z))^{2}z(\frac{\frac{d}{dz}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)}{(1-\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z))})

\displaystyle=-(1-\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z))^{2}z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(1-\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z)).

This implies, since
\ell\mapsto\partial^{\ell}\eta_{x}
is linear and by setting
D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}:={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}-[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}]+\delta[{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}]
(as in Proposition
3.3.3
):

\displaystyle\partial^{D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}\eta_{x}(z)

\displaystyle=\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}\eta_{x}(z)+(1-\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z))^{2}z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(1-\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z))-{\delta z}(1-\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))^{2}\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(1-\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)).

Recall from Proposition
3.3.3
that
x,y
are cyclic c-free in
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
then then are cyclically-free in
(T(\mathcal{A}),{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
). Remark
2.1.5
and Proposition
4.2.2
imply:

(37)

\displaystyle\partial^{D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}\eta_{xy}(z)=\Big(z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm ln}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))\Big)(\partial^{D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}\eta_{x}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))+\Big(z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm ln}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z))\Big)(\partial^{D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}\eta_{y}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)),

(38)

\displaystyle z\frac{(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x})^{\prime}(z)}{\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)}\partial\eta^{D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}\circ\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)=z\frac{(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x})^{\prime}(z)}{\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)}\partial\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}(\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z))

(39)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+(1-\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{xy}(z))^{2}z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(1-\eta_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\omega_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z))-{\delta z}(1-\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{xy}(z))^{2}\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(1-\eta_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z))

Of course, we have a similar relation when
x
is replaced by
y
. By injecting (
38
) in (
37
), we deduce the first formula of the proposition.
∎

As for additive convolution, we specialize the above proposition to two cases; cyclic Boolean independence between
x-1
and
y-1
and cyclic monotone independence between
x-1
and
y
.

4.2.8 Proposition
.

Assume that
x
and
y
are two random variables in a cyclic Boolean probability space

(\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{C}\oplus\overset{\penalty\ \penalty\ \circ}{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})

and assume that
x-1
and
y-1
are cyclic Boolean independent. One has the following relations between the Moments generating functions of
x
,
y
and
xy
with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
:

M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{xy}(z)=M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}(z)+M_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}(z)-z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(\frac{1-z\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z)}{(1-z\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z))(1-z\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y}(z))})-\frac{z}{1-z}

Proof.

From equation (
35
), specializing the equation (
4.2.7
) to the case where
x-1
and
y-1
are Boolean independent among to
\omega_{x}(z)=\omega_{y}(z)=z
in (
4.2.7
) and
\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)=\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)=\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{xy}(z)=z
.
∎

If setting, from a random variable
x
in a cyclic probability space
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
:

\displaystyle\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\Sigma_{x}(z):=M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}(z)+z\frac{d}{dz}\log(1-z\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z))+\frac{z}{1-z},

from Proposition
4.2.8
, we infer the following:

\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\Sigma_{xy}(z)=\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\Sigma_{x}(z)+\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\Sigma_{y}(z)

whenever
x-1
and
y-1
are cyclic Boolean independent. It is instructive to compute
\frac{1}{z}\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\Sigma_{x}(\frac{1}{z})
; straightforward computations yield:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{z}\partial^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\Sigma_{x}(\frac{1}{z})

\displaystyle=G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{x}(z)+\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}(zG^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z))+\frac{1}{z(z-1)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{x}+\frac{1}{z(z-1)},

where we have used the notations from
[
2
]
(we added a superscript). The transform
\mathfrak{h}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}
linearizes
additive
cyclic Boolean convolution and related to the generating series
\mathfrak{c}
of the cyclic Boolean cumulants via

\mathfrak{c}_{x}=\frac{1}{z}\mathfrak{h}_{x}(\frac{1}{z})+z\frac{d}{dz}\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)

see also Section
3.4
.
As in immediate consequence of the above formula, we get the following corollary.

4.2.9 Corollary
.

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
be a sequence of identically distributed elements in a cyclic Boolean probability space, such that
x_{1}-1,\ldots,x_{N}-1
are cyclic Boolean independent. Then:

(40)

M^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{x_{1}\cdots x_{N}}(z)=NM^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{x_{1}}(z)+z\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}\Big(\frac{1-z\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x_{1}}(z)^{N}}{(1-z\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x_{1}}(z))^{N}}\Big)-N\frac{z}{z-1}.

4.2.10 Remark
.

As a final remark about cyclic Boolean multiplicative convolution, we comment on infinite multiplicative infinite divisibility in the case of a unitary operator
u
acting on some finite dimensional Hilbert space
H
. We say that
u
is Boolean multiplicative infinitely divisible if for each integer
n\geq 1
, there exists a finite sequence
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})
of unitaries of
B(H)
, all identically distributed under each of the functional
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
such that
u=u_{1}\cdots u_{n}
. In particular,
u
is Boolean multiplicative infinitely divisible. There is not many of such of operators. In fact, if we assume that
u
has two distinct eigenvalues
e^{{\sf i}b_{1}}
and
e^{{\sf i}b_{2}}
, with multiplicities
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
then the
\eta
-transform
\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}
of
u
has
0
in its range;
\eta_{u}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(\frac{m_{1}}{{\rm dim}(H)}e^{{\sf i}b_{1}}+\frac{m_{2}}{{\rm dim}(H)}e^{{\sf i}b_{2}})=0
. From
[
15
]
, this prevents
u
from being infinitely divisible.

We move on to computing the moment series with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
of two cyclically monotone random variables
x
and
y
.

4.2.11 Proposition
.

Let
x
and
y
be two random variables in a cyclic probability space
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
such that
x-1
is cyclic monotone independent from
y
, then:

(41)

\displaystyle\tilde{M}_{xy}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}(z)=\tilde{M}_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}(z)+(\tilde{M}_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}\circ\eta_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z))(\eta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{y})^{\prime}(z)

where
\tilde{M}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{x}(z)=z^{-1}M_{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z)
.

Proof.

It among to take
\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)=\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z),\omega^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{y}(z)=z
,
\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{xy}(z)=\eta_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z),\eta^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{x}(z)=z,\eta_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)=\eta_{y}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(z)
in the formula of Proposition
4.2.7
and
\delta=1
.
∎

4.3.
Limit theorems

In this section, we utilize the relation (
33
) to prove limit theorems for sums of cyclically conditionally free random variables. For the Central Limit Theorem, we rely on the Central Limits theorems for conditionally free random variables and for infinitesimally free random variables, which we recall now.

4.3.1 Proposition

(Theorem 4.3 in
[
7
]
)
.

Let
(X_{i})_{i\leq 0}
be a sequence of random variables in a conditional probability space
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
identically distributed with respect to both states
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}
and centred. We notate:

\alpha^{2}:={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(X_{1}^{2}),\quad\beta^{2}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(X_{1}^{2}).

As
N
tends to infinity,
S_{N}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}
converges toward an element
s_{\alpha,\beta}
whose
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}
distribution has Cauchy transform:

G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{\alpha^{2},\beta^{2}}(z)=\frac{z(\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}-\alpha^{2})+\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}})}{(z^{2}(\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})-\beta^{4})}

and is distributed under
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
as a semi-circular element with variance
\alpha^{2}
.

We use the notations of the previous proposition.

4.3.2 Proposition

(see Theorem 5.1 in
[
29
]
)
.

Let
(X_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of infinitesimally free, centred identically distributed element of an infinitesimal probability space
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
and set

\alpha^{2}:={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(X_{i}^{2}),\penalty\ \alpha^{\prime}:=\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(X_{1}^{2}).

As
N
tends to infinity,
S_{N}
tends in infinitesimal distribution to an infinitesimal semi-circular element
s^{\prime}_{\alpha^{2},\alpha^{\prime}}
whose infinitesimal Cauchy transform is given by

\partial^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}G_{s_{\alpha^{2},\alpha^{\prime}}}(z):=\partial{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}((z-s_{\alpha^{2},\alpha^{\prime}})^{-1})=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha^{2}}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}-\frac{(z-\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}})}{2\alpha^{2}}).

4.3.3 Theorem
.

Let
(X_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of cyclic c-free random variables in a cyclic-conditional probability space
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
centred and identically distributed. Then, as
N
tends to infinity,
S_{N}
converges in distribution to a random variable
s_{\alpha^{2},\beta^{2},\gamma}
whose distribution has for Cauchy transform with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
:

\displaystyle G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{s_{\alpha,\beta^{2},\gamma}}(z)=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha^{2}}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}-\frac{z-\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}{2\alpha^{2}})-(1-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}}))\frac{1}{\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}-\alpha^{2}+\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\frac{z}{\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}}{z(\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}-\alpha^{2})+\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}-\frac{2z(\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})}{z^{2}(\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})-\beta^{4}}\right),

where
\alpha^{2}={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(X^{2})
,
\beta^{2}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(X^{2})
,
\gamma^{2}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(X_{1}^{2})
and
\alpha^{\prime}=[(1-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}}))\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}+\gamma^{2})].

Before proving the last proposition, let us derive the cyclic Boolean Central Limit Theorem, as in
[
2
]
, Theorem 4.7. Formally, we want to set
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=1
and
\alpha=0,\penalty\ \beta=1,\gamma^{2}=1
which implies
\alpha^{\prime}=\gamma^{2}-1=0

Straightforward computations show that:

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}-\alpha^{2}+\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\frac{z}{\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}}{z(\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}-\alpha^{2})+\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}-\frac{2z(\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})}{z^{2}(\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})-\beta^{4}}=\frac{1}{z}-\frac{2z}{z^{2}-1}+o_{\alpha,0}(1)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}-\frac{z-\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}{2\alpha^{2}}\right)=\alpha^{\prime}(\frac{1}{z^{3}})+o_{\alpha,0}(1).

We obtain, with the notations of
[
2
]
:

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{s_{0,1,\gamma^{2}}}=-\frac{2}{z}+\frac{2z}{z^{2}-1}+\frac{\gamma^{2}-1}{z^{3}}=-\frac{2}{z}+\frac{2z}{z^{2}-1},

which is the cyclic Boolean Central Limit Theorem.

Proof.

We let
S=s_{\alpha^{2},\beta^{2},\gamma}
. Let
S_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n})
, we will denote by
S
its possible limit.
Firstly, with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
,
S_{n}
satisfies the free CLT with variance
\alpha^{2}={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a^{2})
. So

G^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{S_{n}}(z)\to\frac{z-\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}{2\alpha^{2}}=:G^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{S}(z),

and from an easy calculation we get:

-\frac{G^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{S}(z)^{\prime}}{G^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{S}(z)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{z^{2}-\alpha^{2}}}.

Secondly, with respect to the pair
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
,
S_{n}
satisfies the conditionally free CLT. So

G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{S_{n}}(z)\to\frac{z(\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}-\alpha^{2})+1/2\beta^{2}\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}})}{(z^{2}(\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})-\beta^{4})}=:G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{S}(z),

that satisfies

\frac{G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{S}(z)^{\prime}}{G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{S}(z)}=\frac{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}-\alpha^{2}+\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\frac{z}{\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}}{z(\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}-\alpha^{2})+\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}-\frac{2z(\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})}{z^{2}(\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})-\beta^{4}}

Finally,
S_{n}
satisfies an Infinitesimal CLT for
D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
with variance

D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a^{2})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a^{2})-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(a^{2})+(1-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{A})){\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a^{2})=\gamma^{2}-\beta^{2}+(1-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}}))\alpha^{2},

which implies:

G^{D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}_{S_{n}}(z)\to\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}-\frac{z-\sqrt{z^{2}-4\alpha^{2}}}{2\alpha^{2}}.

∎

We say that
a\in(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
is Bernoulli with parameters
((\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}),(\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}},\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}))
,
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\in[0,1]
and
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}},\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}\in\mathbb{R}
in the cyclic-conditional probability space
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})

if it is a Bernoulli element in the conditional probability space
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
and an infinitesimal Bernoulli element in the cyclic probability space
(T(\mathcal{A}),{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
. In particular, for
n\geq 1

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a^{n})=\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}^{n},\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(a^{n})=\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}^{n},

and

D{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a^{n})=\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}^{n}+n\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}^{n-1}\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}},\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(a^{n})=\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}^{n}.

From the formulae:

G_{a}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)=\frac{1-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}}{z}+\frac{\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}}{z-\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},},\penalty\ -\frac{(G_{a}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\prime}(z)}{G_{a}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)}=\frac{1}{z}+\frac{1}{z-\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}},

we easily get that
a
is Bernoulli if and only if, for any integer
n\geq 1
(
\delta=1-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}}
):

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(a^{n})=\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}^{n}(\delta+\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}})-\alpha^{n}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}+n\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}^{n-1}\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}.

4.3.4 Proposition

(Theorem 4.4 in
[
7
]
)
.

Assume that
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(N)N\to\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}
and
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(N)N\to\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}
and take for each
N\geq 1
a sequence
(X^{(N)}_{1},\ldots,X_{N}^{(N)})
of i.i.d conditionally free Bernoulli elements with rates
(\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(N),\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(N))
and
\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}=\alpha_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}=1
. Then,

B^{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}^{(N)}

converges in non-commutative distribution to a conditional free Poisson random variable in
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi})
with parameters
((\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}),(1,1))
:

G_{B^{N}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)\to\mathcal{P}_{\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}}(z):=\frac{1}{2z}\big({z}+(1-\lambda^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})-\sqrt{(z-(1+\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}))^{2}-4\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}})\big)

and

G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}_{B^{N}}(z)\to\mathcal{P}^{c}_{\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}}:=\frac{z(2\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})+\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(1-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\sqrt{(z-(1+\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}))^{2}-4\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}}}{2z(z(\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})+\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(1-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}+\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}))}.

Recall that the free Poisson distribution with parameters
\alpha,\lambda
has Cauchy transform:

\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\lambda}(z)=\frac{z-\alpha(1+\lambda)-\sqrt{(z-\alpha(1+\lambda))^{2}-4\lambda\alpha^{2}}}{2\alpha z}

In comparison with our previous notations,
\mathcal{P}_{1,\lambda}(z)=\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}(z)
.

4.3.5 Proposition

(Corollary 36 in
[
4
]
)
.

In an infinitesimal probability space
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
, for each
N\geq 1
we pick i.i.d Bernoulli elements
(X_{1}^{(N)},\ldots,X_{N}^{(N)})
with rates
\lambda_{\theta}(N)N\to\lambda_{\theta}
and
\lambda_{\theta^{\prime}}(N)N\to\lambda_{\theta^{\prime}}
.
Then
B^{(N)}
converges in distribution toward an infinitesimal Poisson distribution with parameter
(\lambda_{\theta},\lambda_{\theta^{\prime}}
):

G_{B_{N}}^{\theta}(z)\to\mathcal{P}_{\lambda_{\theta}}(z),\penalty\ G_{B_{N}}^{\theta^{\prime}}(z)\to\frac{d}{du}_{|u=0}\mathcal{P}_{1+u,\lambda_{\theta}+\lambda^{\prime}_{\theta}u}(z).

4.3.6 Proposition
.

For each
N\geq 1
, let
X^{(N)}_{1},\ldots,X_{N}^{(N)}
be a finite sequence of size
N
of i.i.d Bernoulli elements in
(\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
. Under the assumptions:

\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(N)N\to\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\quad\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(N)N\to\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}},\quad\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}(N)N\to\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}},

the Cauchy transform under
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
of the sum
B_{N}
of the
X_{i}^{(N)},\penalty\ 1\leq i\leq N
converges toward
G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{p_{\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}}}
that satisfies:

\frac{d}{du}_{|u=0}\mathcal{P}_{1+u,\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}+\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}u}(z)=G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{p_{\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}}}(z)-(1-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}}))\frac{(\mathcal{P}_{\lambda_{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}})^{\prime}(z)}{\mathcal{P}_{\lambda_{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}(z)}+\frac{(\mathcal{P}^{c}_{\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}})^{\prime}(z)}{\mathcal{P}^{c}_{\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}}(z)}.

In the context of cyclic Boolean independence, that is when
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}}=0,\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1_{\mathcal{A}})=0
then:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{c}_{0,\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}}(z)=\frac{1}{z-(1-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})},\quad\frac{d}{dz}{\rm log}\mathcal{P}^{c}_{0,\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}}(z)=-\frac{1}{z-(1-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{z},

\displaystyle\frac{d}{du}_{|u=0}\mathcal{P}_{1+u,\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}u}(z)=-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}\frac{1}{z}+\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}\frac{1}{z-1},

and we thus conclude that

G^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}_{p_{0,\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}},\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}}}(z)=\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}\frac{1}{z(z-1)}+\frac{1}{z(z-(1-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}))}.

We conclude these computations with the following corollary of Proposition
4.3.6
.

4.3.7 Proposition
.

With the notations introduced so far, for each
N\geq 1
we pick cyclic Boolean independent and identically distributed Bernoulli elements
(X_{1}^{(N)},\ldots,X_{N}^{(N)})
in a cyclic probability space
(\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}^{(N)}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}={\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}^{(N)}})
with jump rate parameters
(\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(N),\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}(N))
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}((X_{i}^{(N)})^{n})=\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(N),\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}((X_{i}^{(N)})^{n})=1+\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}(N)-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(N).

Then, under the assumptions that the sequences
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}(N)N
and
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}(N)N
converge respectively toward
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}
and
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}
with
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}\in[0,1[
and
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
, as
N
tends to infinity,

(1)

the
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}}
largest eigenvalues of
B^{(N)}
converge to
1
,

(2)

the following eingenvalue in the decreasing order has, asymptotically, multiplicity one and converges to
1-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}}
,

(3)

the remaining part of the spectrum of
B^{(N)}
converges to
0
.

5.
Products of graphs and cyclic-conditional freeness

In this last section we want to show how some known graph products maybe generalized to include models for the cyclic freeness and for cyclic-conditional freeness. In order to do this, we will work with rooted graphs.

5.1.
Rooted graphs and Adjacency matrices

By a
rooted graph
we understand a pair
(\mathcal{G},e)
, where
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
is a undirected graph with set of vertices

V=V(\mathcal{G})
, and set of edges
E=E(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\{(x,x^{\prime}):x,\ x^{\prime}\in V,\ x\neq x^{\prime}\}
and
e\in V
is
a distinguished vertex called the
root
. For rooted graphs we will use the notation
V^{0}=V\backslash\{e\}
.
Two vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in V
are called
adjacent
if
(v,v^{\prime})\in E
, i.e.
vertices
v,v^{\prime}
are connected with an edge. Then we write
v\sim v^{\prime}
.
The
degree
of
v\in V
is defined by
d(v)=|\{v^{\prime}\in V:v^{\prime}\sim x\}|
. We will assume that
G
is locally finite, that is,
deg(v)<\infty
.

The
adjacency matrix

A=A({\mathcal{G}})
of
{\mathcal{G}}
is the matrix defined by

A_{v,v^{\prime}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&{\rm if}\;\;v\sim v^{\prime}\\
0&{\rm otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

We identify
A
with the densely defined symmetric operator on
l^{2}(V)
defined by

(42)

A\delta(x)=\sum_{v\sim v^{\prime}}\delta(v^{\prime}),

for
v\in V
, where for
\delta(v)
denotes the indicator function on
\{v\}
.

If
(G,e)
is a rooted graph with adjacency matrix
A
acting on
\oplus_{v\in\mathbb{V}(G)}\mathbb{C}v
, the distribution of
G
in the root state is
\mu_{G}\colon\mathbb{C}[X]\to\mathbb{C}
,
\mu(X^{m})=\langle e,A^{n}(e)\rangle
.

5.2.
Star, comb, orthogonal and free products of graphs

For details about the material exposed in the first part of this section, we refer the reader to
[
1
]
.
Let
G_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1})
and
G_{2}=(V_{2},E_{2})
be two
rooted graphs
with distinguished vertices
e_{1}\in V_{1}
and
e_{2}\in V_{2}
.

The
star product
of
G_{1}
with
G_{2}
is the graph
G_{1}\star G_{2}=(V_{1}\times V_{2},E)
such that for
(v_{1},w_{1}),(v_{2},w_{2})\in V_{1}\times V_{2}

the edge
e=(v_{1},w_{1})\sim(v_{2},w_{2})\in E
if and only if one of the following holds:

i)

v_{1}=v_{2}=e_{1}
and
w_{1}\sim w_{2}

ii)

v_{1}\sim v_{2}
and
w_{1}=w_{2}=e_{2}
.

The
comb product
of
G_{1}
with
G_{2}
is the graph
G_{1}\rhd G_{2}=(V_{1}\times V_{2},E)
such that for
(v_{1},w_{1}),(v_{2},w_{2})\in V_{1}\times V_{2}

the edge
e=(v_{1},w_{1})\sim(v_{2},w_{2})\in E
if and only if one of the following holds:

i)

v_{1}=v_{2}
and
w_{1}\sim w_{2}

ii)

v_{1}\sim v_{2}
and
w_{1}=w_{2}=e_{2}
.

A rooted set

(V,e)
is simply a set with distinguished element
e
. For a
countable family
of rooted vertex sets
(V_{i},e_{i})
,
i\in I
, the
free product
of
\{(V_{i},e_{i})\}_{i\in I}
,
is the rooted set
(*_{i\in I}V_{i},e)
, where

*_{i\in I}V_{i}=\{e\}\cup\{v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{m}:v_{k}\in V_{i_{k}}^{0},\ \text{and}\ i_{1}\neq i_{2}\neq\cdots\neq i_{m},\ m\in\mathbb{N}\},

and
e
is the empty word.
The (non-associative)
orthogonal product
of the rooted graphs
(G_{1},e_{1})
and
(G_{2},e_{2})
denoted
G_{1}\vdash G_{2}
resembles the comb product
G_{1}\rhd G_{2}
of the two graphs, to the exception that
G_{2}
is not attached to the root of
G_{1}
;

V(G_{1}\vdash G_{2})={V}(G_{1})\times V(G_{2})
and the set of edges of
G_{1}\vdash G_{2}
consist of the pair of vertices
(v_{1},w_{1}),(v_{2},w_{2})\in V_{1}\times V_{2}

such that one of the following holds:

i)

v_{1}=v_{2}\neq e_{1}
and
w_{1}\sim w_{2}

ii)

v_{1}\sim v_{2}
and
w_{1}=w_{2}=e_{2}
.

The
free product of rooted graphs

(\mathcal{G}_{i},e_{i}),\ i\in I
, is the rooted graph
(*_{i\in I}\mathcal{G}_{i},e)

with vertex set
*_{i\in I}V_{i}
and edge set
*_{i\in I}E_{i}
, defined by

*_{i\in I}E_{i}:=\{(vu,v^{\prime}u):(v,v^{\prime})\in\bigcup_{i\in I}E_{i}\ \text{and}\ u,\ vu,\ v^{\prime}u\in*_{i\in I}V_{i}\}.

We denote this product by
*_{i\in I}(\mathcal{G}_{i},e_{i})
or
*_{i\in I}\mathcal{G}
if no confusion arises.

The free product
G_{1}*G_{2}
decomposes as

{G_{1}*G_{2}}=\Gamma_{1}\star\Gamma_{2}
, where the two rooted graphs
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are the
subordination branches
associated to
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
respectively. Each of the subordination branch is the limit, in the appropriate sense, of a sequence of graphs
(\Gamma_{1}^{(n)})_{n\geq 1}
and
(\Gamma_{2}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
defined by

\Gamma^{(n)}_{1}:=(\Gamma^{(n-1)}_{1}\vdash G_{2})\vdash G_{1},\penalty\ \Gamma^{(1)}:=G_{1}

\Gamma^{(n)}_{2}:=(\Gamma^{(n-1)}_{2}\vdash G_{1})\vdash G_{2},\penalty\ \Gamma^{(2)}:=G_{2}.

5.3.
Free product of graphs and Voiculescu’s Freeness

Let
(G_{1},e_{1})
and
(G_{2},e_{2})
be two rooted graphs. We consider the free product
G_{1}*G_{2}.
of the two graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
. We denote by
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
the spectral distributions in their root state of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
respectively. The spectral distribution in the root state of
G_{1}*G_{2}
is given the free additive convolution
\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}
of the distributions of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.
Moreover, the adjacency matrix
A
of
G_{1}*G_{2}
can be be written as a sum:

A=A^{(1)}+A^{(2)},

with
A^{(1)}
and
A^{(2)}
having distribution
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, respectively, in the root state of
G_{1}\star G_{2}
. Denoting by

{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}:\mathbb{C}\langle X_{1},X_{2}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}

the joint distribution of
(A^{(1)},A^{(2)})
(in the root state), the variables
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are free with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}

[
1
]

5.4.
Conditional free product of graphs

Let
(G_{1},e_{1})
and
(G_{2},e_{2})
be two rooted graphs.
Let
(H_{1},f_{1})
and
(H_{2},f_{2})
be two additional rooted graphs. We now come to a definition of graph product original to this work, to the best of our knowledge. We define
free product of

H_{1}

and

H_{2}

conditionally to

G_{1}

and

G_{2}
by :

H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}:=(H_{1}\vdash\Gamma_{2})\star(H_{2}\vdash\Gamma_{1}).

To build the conditional product we first take the star product of the two graphs
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
. In a second time, to every vertex of
H_{1}\subset H_{1}\star H_{2}
but the root, we attach the subordination branch
\Gamma_{2}
which ’starts’ with the graph
G_{2}
. We repeat this process with
H_{2}
and the subordination branch
\Gamma_{1}
.

Figure 5.
The graph
H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}

Denoting by
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
,
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
the spectral distributions in the root state of
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
respectively, the spectral distribution in the root state of
H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}
is given by

\nu_{1}\prescript{}{\mu_{1}}{\boxplus}_{\mu_{2}}\nu_{2}
, the additive free convolution of
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
conditionally to
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.
Furthermore, the adjacency matrix
A
of
H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}
can be written as:

A=A^{(1)}+A^{(2)},

with
A^{(1)}
and
A^{(2)}
having respective distribution
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
in the root state. If we denote by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}:\mathbb{C}\langle X_{1},X_{2}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}

the joint distribution of
(B^{(1)},B^{(2)})
in the root state, the variables
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are conditionally free with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
. In fact, the matrix
A^{(1)}
is the adjacency matrix of the graph obtained by retaining from the graph
H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}
all vertices and adjacent edges in copies of the two graphs
H_{1}
and
G_{1}
. The matrix
A^{(2)}
is obtained in a similar way, by retaining the edges corresponding to copies of the graphs
H_{2}
and
G_{2}
.
In preparation for the forthcoming section, let us develop the argument that entails conditional freeness of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
with respect to the pair of states
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi})
. Let
\bm{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
be an alternated sequence of polynomials in
\mathbb{C}\langle X_{1},X_{2}\rangle
and suppose that
p_{n}\in\mathbb{C}\langle X_{1}\rangle
(so that
p_{n-1}\in\mathbb{C}\langle X_{2}\rangle
and so on). We assume that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(\pi_{i})=0,
and want to show

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{1}\cdots p_{n})=\langle e,p_{1}(B^{(x)})\cdots p_{n}(B^{(1)})e\rangle=\langle e,p_{1}(B^{(x)})e\rangle\cdots\langle e,p_{n}(B^{(1)})e\rangle

We may write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{1}\cdots p_{n})
as a weighted sum over paths drawn in the graph
H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{1}(B^{(x)})\cdots p_{n}(B^{(1)}))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma:e\to e\end{subarray}}a_{\gamma}(\bm{p},B^{(1)},B^{(2)})

When the path
\gamma
, starting at the root
e
hits a vertex of
H^{1}
that is not the root, it continues exploring vertices of the subordination branch
B^{(1)}
attached to
v
. Besides, in order for
\gamma
to loop at
e
, while passing through
v\in H_{1}\backslash\{e\}
, it must pass a second time through
v
. By denoting
A_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
the adjacency matrix of the subordination branch, and writing it as

A_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}=A^{(1)}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}+A^{(2)}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}

where as usual, we retain in each
A^{(i)}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
only edges in copies of
G_{i}
. Finally, since a path that joins a vertex of
H_{1}
must go through a copy of
G_{2}
, one has the factorization:

\displaystyle\langle e,p_{1}(B^{(x)})\cdots p_{n}(B^{(1)})e\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\subset\mathcal{B}_{1}(v)\\
\gamma:v\to v\end{subarray}}a_{\gamma}({(p_{2},\ldots,p_{n})},A^{(1)}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}},A_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}^{(2)})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{2}\subset H_{1}\\
\gamma_{2}:v\to e\end{subarray}}a_{\gamma_{2}}(p_{1},A_{H_{1}},A_{H_{2}})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+\langle e,p_{1}(B^{(x)})\cdots p_{n-1}(B^{(2)})e\rangle\langle e,p_{n}(B^{(1)}e\rangle

\displaystyle=0+\langle e,p_{1}(B^{(x)})\cdots p_{n-1}(B^{(2)})e\rangle\langle e,p_{n}(B^{(1)}e\rangle.

The polynomials
p_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\langle X_{1},X_{2}\rangle
are alternating and satisfy
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(p_{i})=0
. The last equality follows from freeness. We conclude by induction.

5.5.
Free, star and comb products of graphs from the conditional free product of graphs

We know that free, Boolean and monotone independences are particular cases of conditional freeness. From the graph product side, it is also possible to recover the free product
*
, the star product
\star
and the comb product
\rhd
from the graph product
H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}
.

First from

H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}\simeq(H_{1}\vdash\mathcal{B}_{2})\star(H_{2}\vdash\mathcal{B}_{1}),

if
G_{1}=H_{1}
and
H_{2}=G_{2}
, then
(H_{1}\vdash\mathcal{B}_{2})
and
(H_{1}\vdash\mathcal{B}_{2})
, are just the branches of the free product and hence

H_{1}\prescript{}{H_{1}}{*}_{H_{2}}H_{2}\simeq H_{1}*H_{2}.

For the star and comb product, we note that there is an identity with respect to both of this products, namely
G_{\emptyset}:=(\{e_{1},\emptyset\},e_{1})
, i.e. , the graph with one vertex, satisfying the rules

(G_{1},e_{1})\star G_{\emptyset}\simeq G_{\emptyset}\star(G_{1},e_{1})\simeq(G_{1},e_{1}),

(G_{1},e_{1})*G_{\emptyset}\simeq G_{\emptyset}*(G_{1},e_{1})\simeq(G_{1},e_{1}).

We still have

G_{1}*G_{2}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{1}\star\mathcal{B}_{2}

if we consider that
(G_{1},e_{1})
is the branch of
(G_{1},e_{1})\star G_{\emptyset}\simeq G_{\emptyset}\star(G_{1},e_{1})
subordinate to
(G_{1},e_{1})
and that
G_{\emptyset}
is the branch of
(G_{1},e_{1})\star G_{\emptyset}\simeq G_{\emptyset}\star(G_{1},e_{1})
subordinate to
G_{\emptyset}
. We also define

(G_{1},e_{1})\vdash G_{\emptyset}\simeq(G_{1},e_{1})

and

G_{\emptyset}\vdash(G_{1},e_{1})\simeq G_{\emptyset}.

With this conventions it is clear that

H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{\emptyset}}{*}_{G_{\emptyset}}H_{2}\simeq H_{1}\star H_{2}\ \ \text{and}\ \ H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{\emptyset}}{*}_{H_{2}}H_{2}\simeq H_{1}\rhd H_{2}.

5.6.
Cyclic-conditional freeness and graph products

We assume here that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are finite.
Let us denote by
W
the finite set of vertices of
H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}
which belongs to
H_{1}
or
H_{2}
(the cardinality of
W
is the sum of the number of vertices of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
minus one, because of the identification of the roots). Recall the definition of
A^{(1)}
and
A^{(2)}
of the last section. We define the linear functional

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}:\mathbb{C}\langle X_{1},X_{2}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}

by the sum of vector states given by the vertices in
W
, i.e.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(P)=\sum_{v\in W}\Big\langle P(A^{(1)},A^{(2)})\delta(v),\delta(v)\Big\rangle.

In addition, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, we denote by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{H_{i}}\colon\mathbb{C}\langle X_{i}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}
the distribution of
H_{i}
with respect to the non-normalized trace:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{H_{i}}(P)={\rm Tr}(P(A_{H_{i}})),\quad P\in\mathbb{C}\langle X_{i}\rangle.

5.6.1 Theorem
.

We let
\mathcal{I}
be the two sided ideal of
\mathbb{C}\langle X_{1},X_{2}\rangle
generated by
X_{1},X_{2}
of polynomials with non-constant coefficient.
For each integer
i\in\{1,2\}
and for any polynomial
P(X_{i})\in\mathcal{I}
with non-constant coefficient,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{H_{i}}(P)={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(P)
.
Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
is tracial and the variables
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are cyclic codntionnally free with respect to
({\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega})
.

Proof.

Let us denote by
V_{1}
the set of vertices of
H_{1}
in
H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}
and by
e
the root of
H_{1}\prescript{}{G_{1}}{*}_{G_{2}}H_{2}
. The operator
B^{(1)}
leaves invariant the subspace
\ell^{2}(V_{1})
and vanishes on
\ell^{2}(W\setminus V_{2})
so

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(P(X_{1}))=\sum_{v\in W}\Big\langle P(B^{(1)})\delta(v),\delta(v)\Big\rangle=\sum_{v\in V_{1}}\Big\langle P(B^{(1)})\delta(v),\delta(v)\Big\rangle=Tr_{\ell^{2}(V_{1})}\Big(P(B^{(1)}|_{\ell^{2}(V_{1})})\Big)

as announced. Similarly, the distribution of
X_{2}
with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
is the spectral distribution of
H_{2}
with respect to the non-normalized trace. As a consequence,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
is tracial on the algebra generated by
X_{1}
(respectively on the algebra generated by
X_{2}
).

Now, we claim that, for any alternating tuple
\bm{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
of polynomials with
n\geq 2
, satisfying
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(p_{1})=\cdots={\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}(p_{n})=0
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(p_{n}\cdots p_{1})={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{n})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{1})

if
\bm{p}
is cyclically alternating and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(p_{n}\cdots p_{1})={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{1}p_{n}){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{n-1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{2})

if not. This claim implies the traciality of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}
and the cyclic
c
-freeness.

In order to prove the claim, we denote by
P_{W}
the orthogonal projection to
\ell^{2}(W)
, by
P_{V_{1}}
the orthogonal projection to
\ell^{2}(V_{1})
and by
P_{v}
the orthogonal projection to any vertex
\ell^{2}(\{v\})
. Let us assume that
p_{1}\in\mathbb{C}\langle X_{1}\rangle
(the case
p_{1}\in\mathbb{C}\langle X_{2}\rangle
is treated similarly). We set

B_{1}:=p_{1}(B^{(1)}),\ B_{2}:=p_{2}(B^{(2)}),\ B_{3}:=p_{3}(B^{(1)}),\ B_{4}:=p_{4}(B^{(2)})...

Because of the centring of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
with respect to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.49,0}\psi}
, it is impossible to escape from
\ell^{2}(W)
and come back to
\ell^{2}(W)
by an alternating tuple of operators. Consequently, we have

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(p_{n}\cdots p_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in W}\Big\langle B_{n}\cdots B_{2}B_{1}\delta(v),\delta(v)\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in W}\Big\langle B_{n}P_{W}\cdots P_{W}B_{2}P_{W}B_{1}\delta(v),\delta(v)\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in W}Tr(B_{n}P_{W}\cdots P_{W}B_{2}P_{W}B_{1}P_{v})

\displaystyle=Tr(B_{n}P_{W}\cdots P_{W}B_{2}P_{W}B_{1}P_{W})

Because
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
are centered,
P_{W}B_{1}P_{W}=P_{V_{1}}B_{1}P_{V_{1}}
,
P_{W}B_{2}P_{W}=P_{V_{2}}B_{2}P_{V_{2}}
,
P_{W}B_{3}P_{W}=P_{V_{1}}B_{3}P_{V_{1}}
and so on. As a consequence, and using
P_{V_{1}}P_{V_{2}}=P_{e}
, we get

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(p_{n}\cdots p_{1})

\displaystyle=Tr(B_{n}P_{W}\cdots P_{W}B_{2}P_{W}B_{1}P_{W})

\displaystyle=Tr(P_{V_{2}}B_{n}P_{e}\cdots P_{e}B_{2}P_{e}B_{1}P_{V_{1}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{n-1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{2})Tr(P_{V_{2}}B_{n}P_{e}B_{1}P_{V_{1}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{n-1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{2})Tr(P_{e}B_{n}P_{e}B_{1})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{n})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{1})

if
\bm{p}
is cyclically alternating, and

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(p_{n}\cdots p_{1})

\displaystyle=Tr(B_{n}P_{W}\cdots P_{W}B_{2}P_{W}B_{1}P_{W})

\displaystyle=Tr(P_{V_{1}}B_{n}P_{e}\cdots P_{e}B_{2}P_{e}B_{1}P_{V_{1}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{n-1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{2})Tr(P_{V_{1}}B_{n}P_{e}B_{1}P_{V_{1}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{n-1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{2})Tr(B_{1}P_{V_{1}}B_{n}P_{e})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{n-1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{2}){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varphi}(p_{1}p_{n})

if not.
∎

5.6.2 Remark
.

Note that, from the definition of the conditional free product of graphs,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}(1)=|V(H_{1})|+|V(H_{2})|-1
, whereas
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\omega}_{H_{i}}(1)=|V(H_{i})|
.
In the following particular cases:

H_{1}\prescript{}{\emptyset}{*}_{\emptyset}H_{2}\simeq H_{1}\star H_{2}\ \ \text{and}\ \ H_{1}\prescript{}{\emptyset}{*}_{H_{2}}H_{2}\simeq H_{1}\rhd H_{2},

we recover cyclic Boolean independence and cyclic monotone independence between
A^{(1)}
and
A^{(2)}
. In the case

H_{1}\prescript{}{H_{1}}{*}_{H_{2}}H_{2}\simeq H_{1}*H_{2},

we obtain cyclic freeness (and not infinitesimal freeness, since the root state is not necessarily tracial).
